## A Good Prince / 一位好王子

===Original===

I first saw him one morning of last summer, in the Green Park. Though short, even insignificant, in stature and with an obvious tendency to be obese, he had that unruffled, Olympian air, which is so sure a sign of the Blood Royal. In a suit of white linen he looked serenely cool, despite the heat. Perhaps I should have thought him, had I not been versed in the *Almanach de Gotha*, a trifle older than he is. He did not raise his hat in answer to my salute, but smiled most graciously and made as though he would extend his hand to me, mistaking me, I doubt not, for one of his friends. Forthwith, a member of his suite said something to him in an undertone, whereat he smiled again and took no further notice of me.

I do not wonder the people idolise him. His almost blameless life has been passed among them, nothing in it hidden from their knowledge. When they look upon his dear presentment in the photographer’s window﻿—the shrewd, kindly eyes under the high forehead, the sparse locks so carefully distributed﻿—words of loyalty only and of admiration rise to their lips. For of all princes in modern days he seems to fulfil most perfectly the obligation of princely rank. *Νήπιος* he might have been called in the heroic age, when princes were judged according to their mastery of the sword or of the bow, or have seemed, to those medieval eyes that loved to see a scholar’s pate under the crown, an ignoramus. We are less exigent now. We do but ask of our princes that they should live among us, be often manifest to our eyes, set a perpetual example of a right life. We bid them be the ornaments of our State. Too often they do not attain to our ideal. They give, it may be, a halfhearted devotion to soldiering, or pursue pleasure merely﻿—tales of their frivolity raising now and again the anger of a public swift to envy them their temptations. But against this admirable Prince no such charges can be made. Never (as yet, at least) has he cared to “play at soldiers.” By no means has he shocked the Puritans. Though it is no secret that he prefers the society of ladies, not one breath of scandal has ever tinged his name. Of how many English princes could this be said, in days when Figaro, quill in hand, inclines his ear to every keyhole?

Upon the one action that were well obliterated from his record I need not long insist. It seems that the wife of an aged ex-Premier came to have an audience and pay her respects. Hardly had she spoken when the Prince, in a fit of unreasoning displeasure, struck her a violent blow with his clenched fist. Had His Royal Highness not always stood so far aloof from political contention, it had been easier to find a motive for this unmannerly blow. The incident is deplorable, but it belongs, after all, to an earlier period of his life; and, were it not that no appreciation must rest upon the suppression of any scandal, I should not have referred to it. For the rest, I find no stain, soever faint, upon his life. The simplicity of his tastes is the more admirable for that he is known to care not at all for what may be reported in the newspapers. He has never touched a card, never entered a playhouse. In no stud of racers has he indulged, preferring to the finest blood-horse ever bred a certain white and woolly lamb with a blue riband to its neck. This he is never tired of fondling. It is with him, like the roebuck of Henri Quatre, wherever he goes.

===Chinese===

去年夏天的一个早晨，我在绿园（Green Park）初次见到他。他身量矮小，甚至可以说毫不起眼，又明显有发福之势，却自有一副泰然自若、奥林匹斯诸神般的气派——那是王室血统最确凿不过的标记。时值盛暑，他穿一套白色亚麻衣裳，望之安详而清凉。倘若不是熟谙《哥达年鉴》（*Almanach de Gotha*），我多半会把他看得比实际年纪略长一些。我向他行礼，他并未举帽作答，只极宽和地微微一笑，作势要向我伸出手来——无疑是把我错认作他的一位友人。随即，随行人员中的一位低声对他说了句什么，他便再度一笑，不再理会我了。

民众把他奉为偶像，我毫不奇怪。他这几乎无可指摘的一生就在民众中间度过，一生行状，无一事瞒过世人的耳目。当人们望着摄影师橱窗里他那张可爱的写照——高高的额头下一双精明而慈祥的眼睛，一头稀疏的头发又梳理得那样经心——涌上唇边的，唯有忠爱与赞叹之辞。因为在近世的王子中间，数他似乎最完满地尽到了王族身份的义务。倘在英雄时代——那时王子的优劣全凭剑术弓马定高下——他怕是要落个「婴孩」（*Νήπιος*）的考语；在那些中世纪的眼光里——他们最爱看见王冠底下扣着一颗学者的头颅——他也难免显得胸无点墨。如今我们的要求没那么苛刻了。我们只消王子们生活在民间，常常现于众目之前，为正道的人生树立永久的榜样。我们嘱咐他们做国家的点缀。可惜他们往往够不上这份理想。他们或是对军旅之职半心半意，或是纯粹逐乐——种种浮薄行状的传闻，不时惹起公众的怒火，而这公众，原是动辄艳羡他们那份诱惑的。但对着这位可敬的王子，这类罪状一条也安不上。他从不曾（至少至今不曾）着意「玩当兵」。他也绝无半点开罪清教徒之处。虽说他对女士交际的偏爱已算不得什么秘密，他的名字却从未沾染一丝流言的气息。在费加罗（Figaro）执笔在手、耳朵贴遍每一个锁孔的年月里，能有几位英国王子担得起这句话？

至于那件最好从他的履历里抹去的行止，我不必多费笔墨。事情似乎是这样：一位年迈的前首相夫人前来觐见致敬，她话音未落，王子殿下便在一阵无端发作的不快中，挥起攥紧的拳头，狠狠给了她一记。倘若殿下平素不是远远置身于政争之外，要替这记失礼的拳头寻出一个动机来，原也不难。此事诚然可叹，但终究属于他生平较早的一个阶段；若非任何评鉴都不容建立在隐匿丑闻之上，我本也不愿提起。除此之外，我在他一生中寻不出一丝污点，再轻再淡的也寻不出。他趣味的淡泊愈见可敬，因为尽人皆知，报纸上可能刊出些什么，他全然不放在心上。他从未沾过一张纸牌，从未踏进一家戏园。他也从未豢养过一厩赛马——任凭是人世培育得出的最优秀纯种马，他宁可要一只颈间系着蓝缎带、毛茸茸的小白羊。这只羊羔他爱抚不厌。无论他行至何处，它总相伴左右，恰如亨利四世（Henri Quatre）的那头狍子。

===Original===

Suave and simple his life is! Narrow in range, it may be, but with every royal appurtenance of delight, for to him Love’s happy favours are given and the tribute of glad homage, always, here and there and every other where. Round the flower-garden at Sandringham runs an old wall of red brick, streaked with ivy and topped infrequently with balls of stone. By its iron gates, that open to a vista of flowers, stand two kind policemen, guarding the Prince’s procedure along that bright vista. As his perambulator rolls out of the gate of St. James’s Palace, he stretches out his tiny hands to the scarlet sentinels. An obsequious retinue follows him over the lawns of the White Lodge, cooing and laughing, blowing kisses and praising him. Yet do not imagine his life has been all gaiety! The afflictions that befall royal personages always touch very poignantly the heart of the people, and it is not too much to say that all England watched by the cradle-side of Prince Edward in that dolorous hour, when first the little battlements rose about the rose-red roof of his mouth. I am glad to think that not one querulous word did His Royal Highness, in his great agony, utter. They only say that his loud, incessant cries bore testimony to the perfect lungs for which the House of Hanover is most justly famed. Irreiterate be the horror of that epoch!

As yet, when we know not even what his first words will be, it is too early to predict what verdict posterity will pass upon him. Already he has won the hearts of the people; but, in the years which, it is to be hoped, still await him, he may accomplish more. *Attendons!* He stands alone among European princes﻿—but, as yet, only with the aid of a chair.

London, 1895.

===Chinese===

他的人生何其温婉，何其纯朴！气局或嫌狭小，然而王家的种种赏心乐事一应俱全，因为爱神欣然的恩宠总赐予他，欢欣的敬礼总进献给他——这儿、那儿，还有其余一切地方，无时不然。桑德林汉姆（Sandringham）的花圃四周绕着一道红砖老墙，墙上常春藤痕斑驳，间或顶着一颗颗石球。铁门开处，花径纵深、繁花在望，门前站着两名和善的警察，护卫殿下沿那条明艳的花径徐徐行进。每当他的婴儿车滚出圣詹姆斯宫（St. James's Palace）的大门，他便向那些猩红制服的哨兵伸出小小的双手。一班恭顺的随员跟在他身后走过白屋（White Lodge）的草坪，柔声逗弄，欢声笑语，飞吻连连，赞不绝口。然而切莫以为他的人生尽是欢乐！降临于王室人物身上的磨难，总是深切地触动民众的心；可以毫不夸张地说，当第一批小小的垛堞初初升起在他口腔玫瑰红的穹顶四周的那个悲恸时辰，整个英格兰都守候在爱德华王子（Prince Edward）的摇篮之侧。想到殿下在极度的苦痛中不曾吐出一声怨言，我深感欣慰。人们只是说，他那响亮而不绝于耳的啼哭，为那副完美的肺腑作了见证——汉诺威王朝（House of Hanover）素以这样的肺腑享有盛名，最是当之无愧。愿那一时代的恐怖永不重演！

眼下我们连他的第一句话会是什么都还无从知道，要预言后世将加给他怎样的判词，未免为时过早。他已然赢得了民心；而在那可望仍将迎候着他的岁月里，他或许还能建树更多。*Attendons!*（且让我们静候！）他在欧洲诸王子中间卓然独立——只是至今，还须借一把椅子之力。

伦敦，1895 年。

## Dandies and Dandies / 纨绔子们

===Original===

How very delightful Grego’s drawings are! For all their mad perspective and crude colour, they have indeed the sentiment of style, and they reveal, with surer delicacy than does any other record, the spirit of Mr. Brummell’s day. Grego guides me, as Virgil Dante, through all the mysteries of that other world. He shows me those stiff-necked, over-hatted, wasp-waisted gentlemen, drinking Burgundy in the *Café des Milles Colonnes* or riding through the village of Newmarket upon their fat cobs or gambling at Crockford’s. Grego’s *Green Room of the Opera House* always delights me. The formal way in which Mdlle. Mercandotti is standing upon one leg for the pleasure of Lord Fife and Mr. Ball Hughes; the grave regard directed by Lord Petersham towards that pretty little maid-a-mischief who is risking her rouge beneath the chandelier; the unbridled decorum of Mdlle. Hullin and the decorous debauchery of Prince Esterhazy in the distance, make altogether a quite enchanting picture. But, of the whole series, the most illuminative picture is certainly the *Ball at Almack’s*. In the foreground stand two little figures, beneath whom, on the nether margin, are inscribed those splendid words, “Beau Brummell in Deep Conversation with the Duchess of Rutland.” The Duchess is a girl in pink, with a great wedge-comb erect among her ringlets, the Beau *très dégagé*, his head averse, his chin most supercilious upon his stock, one foot advanced, the gloved fingers of one hand caught lightly in his waistcoat; in fact, the very deuce of a pose.

In this, as in all known images of the Beau, we are struck by the utter simplicity of his attire. The “countless rings,” affected by D’Orsay, the many little golden chains, “every one of them slighter than a cobweb,” that Disraeli loved to insinuate from one pocket to another of his vest, would have seemed vulgar to Mr. Brummell. For is it not to his fine scorn of accessories that we may trace that first aim of modern dandyism, the production of the supreme effect through means the least extravagant? In certain congruities of dark cloth, in the rigid perfection of his linen, in the symmetry of his glove with his hand, lay the secret of Mr. Brummell’s miracles. He was ever most economical, most scrupulous of means. Treatment was everything with him. Even foolish Grace and foolish Philip Wharton, in their book about the beaux and wits of this period, speak of his dressing-room as “a studio in which he daily composed that elaborate portrait of himself which was to be exhibited for a few hours in the clubrooms of the town.” Mr. Brummell was, indeed, in the utmost sense of the word, an artist. No poet nor cook nor sculptor, ever bore that title more worthily than he.

And really, outside his art, Mr. Brummell had a personality of almost Balzacian insignificance. There have been dandies, like D’Orsay, who were nearly painters; painters, like Mr. Whistler, who wished to be dandies; dandies, like Disraeli, who afterwards followed some less arduous calling. I fancy Mr. Brummell was a dandy, nothing but a dandy, from his cradle to that fearful day when he lost his figure and had to flee the country, even to that distant day when he died, a broken exile, in the arms of two *religieuses*. At Eton, no boy was so successful as he in avoiding that strict alternative of study and athletics which we force upon our youth. He once terrified a master, named Parker, by asserting that he thought cricket “foolish.” Another time, after listening to a reprimand from the headmaster, he twitted that learned man with the asymmetry of his neckcloth. Even in Oriel he could see little charm, and was glad to leave it, at the end of his first year, for a commission in the Tenth Hussars. Crack though the regiment was﻿—indeed, all the commissions were granted by the Regent himself﻿—young Mr. Brummell could not bear to see all his brother-officers in clothes exactly like his own; was quite as deeply annoyed as would be some god, suddenly entering a restaurant of many mirrors. One day, he rode upon parade in a pale blue tunic, with silver epaulettes. The Colonel, apologising for the narrow system which compelled him to so painful a duty, asked him to leave the parade. The Beau saluted, trotted back to quarters and, that afternoon, sent in his papers. Henceforth he lived freely as a fop, in his maturity, should.

===Chinese===

格雷戈（Grego）的画真是妙极了！尽管透视狂放、设色粗率，它们却确乎怀有一种风格的情致，而且比任何别的记载都更精微可靠地揭示出布鲁梅尔先生那个时代的精神。格雷戈引着我，如维吉尔引但丁一般，遍历那个另一世界的种种奥秘。他让我看见那些脖颈僵直、帽子高耸、蜂腰紧束的先生们，正在「千柱咖啡馆」（Café des Milles Colonnes）里饮勃艮第酒，或骑着肥壮的矮种马穿过纽马基特村，或在克罗克福德（Crockford's）赌场里一掷千金。格雷戈那幅《歌剧院后台》（Green Room of the Opera House）总教我心喜：梅尔康多蒂小姐（Mdlle. Mercandotti）为博法夫勋爵（Lord Fife）与鲍尔·休斯先生（Mr. Ball Hughes）之欢，单腿伫立，仪态俨然；彼得沙姆勋爵（Lord Petersham）目光庄重，投向枝形吊灯下那个拿胭脂冒险的俏皮小淘气；远处，于兰小姐（Mdlle. Hullin）端庄得放纵不羁，埃斯特哈齐亲王（Prince Esterhazy）放纵得端庄可掬——合起来恰成一幅迷人的画。但在整个系列里，最具启发性的一幅无疑要数《阿尔马克舞会》（Ball at Almack's）。前景里立着两个小小人影，他们脚下的画缘上题着那句堂皇的说明文字：「美男子布鲁梅尔与拉特兰公爵夫人（Duchess of Rutland）倾心深谈」。公爵夫人是个穿粉红衣裳的少女，鬈发间高高竖着一把楔形大梳；「美男子」（the Beau）则好一副洒脱不羁（très dégagé）的架势——头颅微侧，下巴倨傲地抵着领巾，一足前伸，一只手戴着手套的指头轻轻勾住马甲；实实在在，是一副神气透顶、妙到毫巅的姿势。

在这幅画里，正如在「美男子」一切已知的造像里一样，最先令人一惊的是他衣饰的全然素朴。德·奥赛（D'Orsay）偏爱的那些「数不清的戒指」，迪斯累里爱从马甲的这个口袋穿进那个口袋的许多条小金链——「每一条都比蛛丝还细」——在布鲁梅尔先生眼里都会显得俗气。因为，近代纨绔作风的第一宗旨——以最不铺张的手段收至高之效——岂不正可以溯源于他对随身零碎物的精妙鄙夷么？深色布料间的某种谐调，亚麻衣衫一丝不苟的完美，手套与手形的对称无间——布鲁梅尔先生种种奇迹的秘密即在于此。他对手段永远最为俭省、最为讲究。处理便是一切。连愚蠢的格雷斯和愚蠢的菲利普·沃顿（Grace and Philip Wharton），在他们那部谈论这个时代的纨绔子与才子的书里，也说他的更衣室是「一间画室，他每天在里面创作他那幅精工的自画像，供城中各俱乐部陈列几个小时」。布鲁梅尔先生，就这个词至极的意义而言，的确是一位艺术家。无论诗人、厨师还是雕塑家，谁承受这个名号都不曾比他更当之无愧。

而且说实在的，在他的艺术之外，布鲁梅尔先生的个性小到几乎巴尔扎克式的无足轻重。有过这样的纨绔子，像德·奥赛，差点成了画家；有过这样的画家，像惠斯勒先生，一心想做纨绔子；有过这样的纨绔子，像迪斯累里，后来改行从事了些不那么艰辛的营生。我倒觉得布鲁梅尔先生从摇篮直到那可怕的一天——他坏了身段、不得不逃离国门——乃至直到那遥远的一天——他潦倒流亡，死在两名修女（religieuses）的臂弯里——始终是个纨绔子，也仅仅是个纨绔子。在伊顿，没有哪个少年像他那样成功地躲开了我们强加给青年的那道非用功即运动的严苛抉择。他有一回宣称板球「愚蠢」，把一位名叫帕克（Parker）的师傅吓了一跳。又有一回，他听完校长的一顿训斥，反唇讥诮那位学问家领巾系得不对称。就连奥里尔学院（Oriel）他也看不出多少意趣，乐得在第一年念完便离开，去第十骠骑兵团（Tenth Hussars）谋了个军职。团队固然是精锐之师——那些委任状还都是摄政王亲自颁下的——年轻的布鲁梅尔先生却受不了眼见全体同僚军官穿得同自己一模一样；其懊恼之深，不啻某位天神骤然闯进一家四壁皆镜的餐馆。一天，他穿一件缀银肩章的浅蓝色军衣骑马赴操。上校为他所不得不遵奉的拘狭军制致歉，请他退操。「美男子」行了个礼，策马小跑回营，当天下午便递上了辞呈。从此他自由自在地过活，一如一位盛年的纨绔子所应享的那份自由。

===Original===

His *début* in the town was brilliant and delightful. Tales of his elegance had won for him there a precedent fame. He was reputed rich. It was known that the Regent desired his acquaintance. And thus, Fortune speeding the wheels of his cabriolet and Fashion running to meet him with smiles and roses in St. James’s, he might well, had he been worldly or a weakling, have yielded his soul to the polite follies. But he passed them by. Once he was settled in his suite, he never really strayed from his toilet-table, save for a few brief hours. Thrice every day of the year did he dress, and three hours were the average of his every toilet, and other hours were spent in council with the cutter of his coats or with the custodian of his wardrobe. A single, devoted life! To White’s, to routs, to races, he went, it is true, not reluctantly. He was known to have played battledore and shuttlecock in a moonlit garden with Mr. Previté and some other gentlemen. His elopement with a young Countess from a ball at Lady Jersey’s was quite notorious. It was even whispered that he once, in the company of some friends, made as though he would wrench the knocker off the door of some shop. But these things he did, not, most certainly, for any exuberant love of life. Rather did he regard them as healthful exercise of the body and a charm against that dreaded corpulency which, in the end, caused his downfall. Some recreation from his work even the most strenuous artist must have; and Mr. Brummell naturally sought his in that exalted sphere whose modish elegance accorded best with his temperament, the sphere of *le plus beau monde*. General Bucknall used to growl, from the window of the Guards’ Club, that such a fellow was only fit to associate with tailors. But that was an old soldier’s fallacy. The proper associates of an artist are they who practise his own art rather than they who﻿—however honourably﻿—do but cater for its practice. For the rest, I am sure that Mr. Brummell was no lackey, as they have suggested. He wished merely to be seen by those who were best qualified to appreciate the splendour of his achievements. Shall not the painter show his work in galleries, the poet flit down Paternoster Row? Of rank, for its own sake, Mr. Brummell had no love. He patronised all his patrons. Even to the Regent his attitude was always that of a master in an art to one who is sincerely willing and anxious to learn from him.

Indeed, English society is always ruled by a dandy, and the more absolutely ruled the greater that dandy be. For dandyism, the perfect flower of outward elegance, is the ideal it is always striving to realise in its own rather incoherent way. But there is no reason why dandyism should be confused, as it has been by nearly all writers, with mere social life. Its contact with social life is, indeed, but one of the accidents of an art. Its influence, like the scent of a flower, is diffused unconsciously. It has its own aims and laws, and knows none other. And the only person who ever fully acknowledged this truth in aesthetics is, of all persons most unlikely, the author of *Sartor Resartus*. That anyone who dressed so very badly as did Thomas Carlyle should have tried to construct a philosophy of clothes has always seemed to me one of the most pathetic things in literature. He in the Temple of Vestments! Why sought he to intrude, another Clodius, upon those mysteries and light his pipe from those ardent censers? What were his hobnails that they should mar the pavement of that delicate Temple? Yet, for that he betrayed one secret rightly heard there, will I pardon his sacrilege. “A dandy,” he cried through the mask of Teufelsdröck, “is a clothes-wearing man, a man whose trade, office, and existence consists in the wearing of clothes. Every faculty of his soul, spirit, purse, and person is heroically consecrated to this one object, the wearing of clothes wisely and well.” Those are true words. They are, perhaps, the only true words in *Sartor Resartus*. And I speak with some authority. For I found the key to that empty book, long ago, in the lock of the author’s empty wardrobe. His hat, that is still preserved in Chelsea, formed an important clue.

But (behold!) as we repeat the true words of Teufelsdröck, there comes Monsieur Barbey D’Aurevilly, that gentle *moqueur*, drawling, with a wave of his hand, “*Les esprits qui ne voient pas les choses que par leur plus petit côté, ont imaginé que le Dandysme était surtout l’art de la mise, une heureuse et audacieuse dictature en fait de toilette et d’élégance extérieure. Très-certainement c’est cela aussi, mais c’est bien d’avantage. Le Dandysme est toute une manière d’être et l’on n’est pas que par la côté matériellement visible. C’est une manière d’être entièrement composée de nuances, comme il arrive toujours dans les sociétés très-vieilles et très-civilisées.*” It is a pleasure to argue with so suave a subtlist, and we say to him that this comprehensive definition does not please us. We say we think he errs.

===Chinese===

他在城中初次亮相，既辉煌又叫人欢喜。他风雅的种种传说已先为他赢得了声名。据说他家道殷实。尽人皆知摄政王愿与他结交。于是，命运女神催着他双轮轻马车的轮子，时髦女神满面含笑、手捧玫瑰，赶到圣詹姆斯来迎他——他倘若是个俗人，或是个软骨头，原大可把灵魂拱手交给那些文雅的痴愚。但他对它们视若无睹，扬长而过。一旦在自己的那套房间里安顿下来，除了短短几个钟头，他实际上从不曾真正离开他的梳妆台。一年之中他每日更衣三次，每次梳妆平均三个钟头，其余的时辰则用来同他的外衣裁缝或他的衣橱总管议事。一种专一而虔诚的生活！去怀特俱乐部（White's），去盛会，去赛马场，他确实去得不甚勉强。人尽皆知，他曾与普雷维泰先生（Mr. Previté）及其他几位绅士在一个月色溶溶的园子里打板羽球。他携一位年轻伯爵夫人从泽西夫人（Lady Jersey）的舞会上私奔一事，更是相当有名。甚至有人窃窃私语，说他有一回在几位友人簇拥下，作势要拧下某家铺子门上的门环。但他做这些事，最最肯定，绝不是为了对人生怀有什么过盛的爱。毋宁说，他把它们看作有益身体的运动，看作抵御那可怕的发福的一道符咒——而发福，到头来正是他倒台的原因。再奋发的艺术家也得有劳作之余的消遣；布鲁梅尔先生自然去那个最高的圈子里寻他的消遣——那个圈子的时新雅致最合他的气质，也就是上流社会（le plus beau monde）的圈子。巴克纳尔将军（General Bucknall）惯于从禁卫军俱乐部的窗口咆哮，说这种家伙只配同裁缝们为伍。但那是老军人的谬见。一位艺术家合适的同侪，是操同一门艺术的人，而非那些只是替这门艺术的实践办采买的人——不管办得多么体面。至于其余，我敢肯定，布鲁梅尔先生绝不像他们所影射的那样是个仆从。他不过是想被那些最有资格鉴赏他成就之辉煌的人看见罢了。画家岂不应当在画廊里陈列作品，诗人岂不应当翩然飞下帕特诺斯特街？对于地位本身，布鲁梅尔先生并无恋慕。他对他的各位恩主，一概以恩主自居。连对摄政王，他的态度始终是一门艺术的宗师对待一个诚心诚意、求教若渴的学生的态度。

的确，英国社会永远由一位纨绔子统治着，而那位纨绔子越伟大，统治便越绝对。因为纨绔作风——外在优雅的完满之花——正是英国社会以自己那相当散漫无章的方式始终竭力实现的理想。但没有任何理由把纨绔作风同单纯的社交生活混为一谈，虽然几乎所有作家都这么混同了。它与社交生活的接触，实在不过是一门艺术的种种际遇之一。它的影响，如花的芬芳，于不知不觉间播散。它有自己的宗旨与法则，此外一概不认。而在美学上完整承认这一真理的唯一一人，偏偏是最不可能的一人——《旧衣新裁》（Sartor Resartus）的作者。一个穿得像托马斯·卡莱尔（Thomas Carlyle）那样糟糕的人，居然试图建造一套衣服的哲学，这在我想来始终是文学中最可悲的事情之一。他，居然置身衣裳的圣殿！他何苦学克洛狄乌斯（Clodius）再来闯一回那些秘仪，从那些炽烈的香炉上点他的烟斗？他的平头钉鞋算什么东西，也配磨损那座精微圣殿的地坪？然而，念他泄漏了一句在那里窃听得的真言，我愿饶恕他的渎圣。「一个纨绔子，」他借托伊费尔斯德罗克（Teufelsdröck）的面具喊道，「是一个穿衣服的人，一个以穿衣服为业、为职、为生存的人。他灵魂、精神、钱袋与肉身的每一分才能，都英勇地奉献给这一个目标：把衣服穿得又明智、又妥帖。」这些是真实不虚的话。它们或许正是《旧衣新裁》里仅有的真实不虚的话。而我这话带着几分权威。因为多年以前，我正是在那本书作者那口空衣橱的锁孔里，找到了那本空洞之书的钥匙。他至今还保存在切尔西的那顶帽子，便是一条重要的线索。

但是（看哪！）正当我们复诵着托伊费尔斯德罗克这些真实的话语，巴尔贝·多尔维利先生（Monsieur Barbey D'Aurevilly）来了——那位温文的嘲弄家（moqueur）——拖长了声音，把手一挥道：「*Les esprits qui ne voient pas les choses que par leur plus petit côté, ont imaginé que le Dandysme était surtout l'art de la mise, une heureuse et audacieuse dictature en fait de toilette et d'élégance extérieure. Très-certainement c'est cela aussi, mais c'est bien d'avantage. Le Dandysme est toute une manière d'être et l'on n'est pas que par la côté matériellement visible. C'est une manière d'être entièrement composée de nuances, comme il arrive toujours dans les sociétés très-vieilles et très-civilisées.*」（大意：那些只从事物最小一面看事物的头脑，遂臆想纨绔作风主要是一种着装的艺术，是梳妆与外表风雅方面一次侥幸而大胆的独裁。诚然也是这个，但远不止此。纨绔作风是一整套存在的方式，而人之成为此等人物，并非仅凭物质可见的一面。这是一种全然由精微层次构成的存在方式，一如在那些极其古老、极其文明的社会里向来发生的情形。）同一位如此温文的精微论客争辩是一桩乐事，我们对他说：这个包罗万象的定义不合我们的意。我们说，我们认为他错了。

===Original===

Not that Monsieur’s analysis of the dandiacal mind is worthless by any means. Nor, when he declares that George Brummell was the supreme king of the dandies and *fut le dandysme même*, can I but piously lay one hand upon the brim of my hat, the other upon my heart. But it is as an artist, and for his supremacy in the art of costume, and for all he did to gain the recognition of costume as in itself an art, and for that superb taste and subtle simplicity of mode whereby he was able to expel, at length, the Byzantine spirit of exuberance which had possessed St. James’s and wherefore he is justly called the Father of Modern Costume, that I do most deeply revere him. It is not a little strange that Monsieur D’Aurevilly, the biographer who, in many ways, does seem most perfectly to have understood Mr. Brummell, should belittle to a mere phase that which was indeed the very core of his existence. To analyse the temperament of a great artist and then to declare that his art was but a part﻿—a little part﻿—of his temperament, is a foolish proceeding. It is as though a man should say that he finds, on analysis, that gunpowder is composed of potassium chloride (let me say), nitrate and power of explosion. Dandyism is ever the outcome of a carefully cultivated temperament, not part of the temperament itself. That *manière d’être, entièrement composée de nuances*, was not more, as the writer seems to have supposed, than attributory to Mr. Brummell’s art. Nor is it even peculiar to dandies. All delicate spirits, to whatever art they turn, even if they turn to no art, assume an oblique attitude towards life. Of all dandies, Mr. Brummell did most steadfastly maintain this attitude. Like the single-minded artist that he was, he turned full and square towards his art and looked life straight in the face out of the corners of his eyes.

It is not hard to see how, in the effort to give Mr. Brummell his due place in history, Monsieur D’Aurevilly came to grief. It is but strange that he should have fallen into a rather obvious trap. Surely he should have perceived that, so long as Civilisation compels her children to wear clothes, the thoughtless multitude will never acknowledge dandyism to be an art. If considerations of modesty or hygiene compelled everyone to stain canvas or chip marble every morning, painting and sculpture would in like manner be despised. Now, as these considerations do compel everyone to envelop himself in things made of cloth and linen, this common duty is confounded with that fair procedure, elaborate of many thoughts, in whose accord the fop accomplishes his toilet, each morning afresh, Aurora speeding on to gild his mirror. Not until nudity be popular will the art of costume be really acknowledged. Nor even then will it be approved. Communities are ever jealous (quite naturally) of the artist who works for his own pleasure, not for theirs﻿—more jealous by far of him whose energy is spent only upon the glorification of himself alone. Carlyle speaks of dandyism as a survival of “the primeval superstition, self-worship.” “*La vanité*,” are almost the first words of Monsieur D’Aurevilly, “*c’est un sentiment contre lequel tout le monde est impitoyable.*” Few remember that the dandy’s vanity is far different from the crude conceit of the merely handsome man. Dandyism is, after all, one of the decorative arts. A fine ground to work upon is its first postulate. And the dandy cares for his physical endowments only in so far as they are susceptible of fine results. They are just so much to him as to the decorative artist is inilluminate parchment, the form of a white vase or the surface of a wall where frescoes shall be.

Consider the words of Count D’Orsay, spoken on the eve of some duel, “We are not fairly matched. If I were to wound him in the face it would not matter; but if he were to wound me, *ce serait vraiment dommage*!” There we have a pure example of a dandy’s peculiar vanity﻿—“It would be a real pity!” They say that D’Orsay killed his man﻿—no matter whom﻿—in this duel. He never should have gone out. Beau Brummell never risked his dandyhood in these mean encounters. But D’Orsay was a wayward, excessive creature, too fond of life and other follies to achieve real greatness. The power of his predecessor, the Father of Modern Costume, is over us yet. All that is left of D’Orsay’s art is a waistcoat and a handful of rings﻿—vain relics of no more value for us than the fiddle of Paganini or the mask of Menischus! I think that in Carolo’s painting of him, we can see the strength, that was the weakness, of *le jeune Cupidon*. His fingers are closed upon his cane as upon a sword. There is mockery in the inconstant eyes. And the lips, so used to close upon the wine-cup, in laughter so often parted, they do not seem immobile, even now. Sad that one so prodigally endowed as he was, with the three essentials of a dandy﻿—physical distinction, a sense of beauty and wealth or, if you prefer the term, credit﻿—should not have done greater things. Much of his costume was merely showy or eccentric, without the rotund unity of the perfect fop’s. It had been well had he lacked that dash and spontaneous gallantry that make him cut, it may be, a more attractive figure than Beau Brummell. The youth of St. James’s gave him a wonderful welcome. The flight of Mr. Brummell had left them as sheep without a shepherd. They had even cried out against the inscrutable decrees of fashion and curtailed the height of their stocks. And (lo!) here, ambling down the Mall with tasselled cane, laughing in the window at White’s or in Fop’s Alley posturing, here, with the devil in his eyes and all the graces at his elbow, was D’Orsay, the prince paramount who should dominate London and should guard life from monotony by the daring of his whims. He accepted so many engagements that he often dressed very quickly both in the morning and at nightfall. His brilliant genius would sometimes enable him to appear faultless, but at other times not even his fine figure could quite dispel the shadow of a toilet too hastily conceived. Before long he took that fatal step, his marriage with Lady Harriet Gardiner. The marriage, as we all know, was not a happy one, though the wedding was very pretty. It ruined the life of Lady Harriet and of her mother, the Blessington. It won the poor Count further still further from his art and sent him spinning here, there, and everywhere. He was continually at Cleveden, or Belvoir, or Welbeck, laughing gaily as he brought down our English partridges, or at Crockford’s, smiling as he swept up our English guineas from the board. Holker declares that, excepting Mr. Turner, he was the finest equestrian in London and describes how the mob would gather every morning round his door to see him descend, insolent from his toilet, and mount and ride away. Indeed, he surpassed us all in all the exercises of the body. He even essayed preeminence in the arts (as if his own art were insufficient to his vitality!) and was forever penning impenuous verses for circulation among his friends. There was no great harm in this, perhaps. Even the handwriting of Mr. Brummell was not unknown in the albums. But D’Orsay’s painting of portraits is inexcusable. The aesthetic vision of a dandy should be bounded by his own mirror. A few crayon sketches of himself﻿—*dilectissimae imagines*﻿—are as much as he should ever do. That D’Orsay’s portraits, even his much-approved portrait of the Duke of Wellington, are quite amateurish, is no excuse. It is the process of painting which is repellent; to force from little tubes of lead a glutinous flamboyance and to defile, with the hair of a camel therein steeped, taut canvas, is hardly the diversion for a gentleman; and to have done all this for a man who was admittedly a field-marshal﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

这并不是说，这位先生对纨绔心理的分析毫无可取。而当他宣告乔治·布鲁梅尔是纨绔子们至高无上的王、正是纨绔作风的化身（fut le dandysme même）时，我也只能虔诚地一手按住帽檐、一手按住心口。但我之深深敬奉他，是作为一个艺术家：为他在此衣装艺术中的至高地位，为他为使衣装本身被公认为一门艺术所做的一切，为那种精妙绝伦的趣味与素朴入微的式样——正是凭着它们，他终于驱逐了盘踞圣詹姆斯的拜占庭式的繁缛风气，也正因此，他被公正地尊为现代衣装之父。说来颇为蹊跷：多尔维利先生这位在许多方面似乎最透彻地理解了布鲁梅尔先生的传记作者，竟把那件实为布鲁梅尔生命核心的事情，贬低成区区一个阶段。剖析一位伟大艺术家的气质，然后宣称他的艺术不过是其气质的一部分——一小部分——乃是一种愚蠢的做法。这就好比一个人宣布：他经分析发现，火药是由氯化钾（姑且这么说吧）、硝酸盐和爆炸力构成的。纨绔作风永远是一种精心养就的气质所结的果，而非气质本身的一部分。那「一整套由精微层次构成的存在方式」（manière d'être, entièrement composée de nuances），并不像这位作者似乎设想的那样，仅仅是布鲁梅尔先生艺术的附属物。它甚至并非纨绔子们所独有。一切精微的心灵，无论投身哪一门艺术，甚至不投身任何艺术，对人生都取一种斜睨的姿态。而在所有纨绔子当中，最坚定不移地保持这一姿态的，正是布鲁梅尔先生。像他那种心地纯粹的艺术家一样，他端端正正地面向他的艺术，却拿眼角的余光直视人生。

不难看出，多尔维利先生在努力还布鲁梅尔先生以历史上应有之位时是怎么栽的跟头。蹊跷的只是，他竟掉进了一个相当显眼的陷阱。他本该看出：只要文明还强迫她的儿女们穿衣服，不动脑筋的芸芸众生就永远不会承认纨绔作风是一门艺术。倘若羞耻或卫生的考虑强迫人人每天早晨往画布上涂抹、往大理石上凿屑，绘画与雕塑也照样会遭人白眼。而如今，正是这些考虑强迫人人把自己裹进布匹和亚麻制成的东西里，于是这桩人人有份的差事，便同那套美妙的程序混为一谈了——那是一套酝酿着许多心思的精工程序，纨绔子每天清晨焕然一新地循着它完成梳妆，奥罗拉女神疾驰而来，为他的镜子镀金。非得等到裸体蔚然成风，衣装的艺术才会被真正承认。而即便到那时，它也不会被赞许。公众社会对于那些只为自己快活、不为他们快活而创作的艺术家，从来心怀妒忌（这是顶自然的）——对那位把精力只花在为自己一人增光添彩上的艺术家，妒忌得更要厉害得多。卡莱尔说纨绔作风是「太古的迷信——自我崇拜——」的遗存。「*La vanité*（虚荣），」多尔维利先生开卷几乎头一句话便是，「*c'est un sentiment contre lequel tout le monde est impitoyable*（人人对之都不留情面的一种情感）。」很少有人记得，纨绔子的虚荣，同单单一个漂亮男人的粗卤自负大异其趣。纨绔作风毕竟是一门装饰艺术。一块精良的底子是它的第一公设。纨绔子看重自己的天生丽质，也只看重到它们堪出精妙成品的地步。它们在他眼里的分量，恰似未经彩饰的羊皮纸、一只白瓶的器形、有待湿壁画的墙面在装饰艺术家眼里的分量。

想想德·奥赛伯爵在一场决斗前夜说的话：「我们俩配得并不公平。我若伤了他的脸，那不要紧；可他若伤了我，*ce serait vraiment dommage*（那可真就可惜了）！」这里我们便得到一个纨绔子特有之虚荣的纯粹标本——「那可真就可惜了！」据说德·奥赛在那场决斗里杀了他的对手——且不管是哪一位——他本来就不该去的。「美男子」布鲁梅尔从不曾在这类无聊的交锋里拿自己的纨绔身份去冒险。但德·奥赛是个任性而过火的人物，太爱人生、也太爱其余种种痴愚，成不了真正的大器。他的前辈、现代衣装之父的威权，至今仍笼罩着我们。德·奥赛的艺术所剩下的，只有一件马甲和一小把戒指——虚妄的遗物，于我们并不比帕格尼尼（Paganini）的小提琴或梅尼苏斯（Menischus）的面具更值钱！我想，在卡罗洛（Carolo）为他画的那幅像里，我们看得出「小丘比特」（le jeune Cupidon）的那种力量，而那力量正是他的弱点。他的手指扣着手杖，如同扣着一柄剑。那双变幻不定的眼睛里含着揶揄。还有那两片惯于拢住酒杯、惯于在朗笑中时时开启的嘴唇，即便此刻看上去也并未僵止。可叹啊，一个像他这样得天独厚、集纨绔子三要素——出众的仪表、对美的感受力、财富，或者换个你喜欢的字眼：信用——于一身的人，竟不曾有更大的建树。他的衣装有许多只落得炫异矜奇，缺少完美纨绔子那种浑圆的统一。倘若他没有那股冲劲、没有那天生天化的风流——正是这些使他兴许比「美男子」布鲁梅尔更讨人喜欢——那反倒好了。圣詹姆斯的青年们给了他一场盛大的欢迎。布鲁梅尔先生的出走，使他们成了没牧人的羊。他们甚至一度大声抱怨时髦那不可测度的敕令，把领巾的高度都削减了。看哪！这位执穗饰手杖沿林荫路（the Mall）缓辔而行的，这位在怀特俱乐部的窗口朗声而笑、或在「纨绔巷」（Fop's Alley）里顾影摆姿的，这位眼中住着魔鬼、肘边簇拥着群芳的，正是德·奥赛——那将要主宰伦敦、将要凭他异想之大胆守护人生使免于单调的无上君侯。他应下的约会太多，以致清晨和傍晚常常匆匆着装。他辉煌的天才有时能使他显得无懈可击，但另一些时候，连他的好身段也驱不散一套草草构思的梳妆投下的阴影。不久他迈出了那致命的一步——与哈丽特·加德纳小姐（Lady Harriet Gardiner）成婚。这桩婚姻，众所周知，并不美满，虽然婚礼办得十分好看。它毁掉了哈丽特小姐和她母亲布莱辛顿伯爵夫人（the Blessington）的一生，也把可怜的伯爵拖得离他的艺术愈来愈远，害得他滴溜溜打转，东一处、西一处、处处奔波。他不断地做客于克莱夫登、贝尔沃或韦尔贝克，欢欢喜喜笑着击落我们的英国鹧鸪；或是坐在克罗克福德赌场里，含笑把我们的英国几尼从赌台上尽数扫进怀中。霍尔克（Holker）宣称，除特纳先生（Mr. Turner）之外，他是伦敦最出色的骑手，还描写每天清晨人群如何聚集在他门前，看他梳妆方罢、睥睨自得地走下楼来，上马扬长而去。的确，在一切身体的技艺上，他都胜过我们所有人。他甚至试图在诸门艺术里也争个出类拔萃（仿佛他自己那门艺术还不够他施展生机似的），永远在写些慷慨激昂的诗句，供友朋传阅。这或许没有多大害处。连布鲁梅尔先生的笔迹，在纪念册里也不算罕见。但德·奥赛去画肖像，就是不可原谅的了。一个纨绔子的审美视野，应当以他自己的镜子为疆界。画几幅自己的粉笔速写——最亲爱的影像（dilectissimae imagines）——已是他至多该做的了。德·奥赛的肖像画，连他那幅备受称道的威灵顿公爵（Duke of Wellington）像也算上——纯属业余，这并不是什么辩解。可憎的正是作画这道工序本身：从一支支小铅管里挤压出黏腻的浮艳，再用在管中蘸饱的驼毛去玷污绷紧的画布——这几乎算不得绅士的消遣；何况这一切，还是为一位公认的陆军元帅干的……

===Original===

I have often thought that this selfish concentration, which is a part of dandyism, is also a symbol of that *einsamkeit* felt in greater or less degree by the practitioners of every art. But, curiously enough, the very unity of his mind with the ground he works on exposes the dandy to the influence of the world. In one way dandyism is the least selfish of all the arts. Musicians are seen and, except for a price, not heard. Only for a price may you read what poets have written. All painters are not so generous as Mr. Watts. But the dandy presents himself to the nation whenever he sallies from his front door. Princes and peasants alike may gaze upon his masterpieces. Now, any art which is pursued directly under the eye of the public is always far more amenable to fashion than is an art with which the public is but vicariously concerned. Those standards to which artists have gradually accustomed it the public will not see lightly set at naught. Very rigid, for example, are the traditions of the theatre. If my brother were to declaim his lines at the Haymarket in the florotund manner of Macready, what a row there would be in the gallery! It is only by the impalpable process of evolution that change comes to the theatre. Likewise in the sphere of costume no swift rebellion can succeed, as was exemplified by the Prince’s effort to revive knee-breeches. Had his Royal Highness elected, in his wisdom, to wear tight trousers strapped under his boots, “smalls” might, in their turn, have reappeared, and at length﻿—who knows?﻿—knee-breeches. It is only by the trifling addition or elimination, modification or extension, made by this or that dandy and copied by the rest, that the mode proceeds. The young dandy will find certain laws to which he must conform. If he outrage them he will be hooted by the urchins of the street, not unjustly, for he will have outraged the slowly constructed laws of artists who have preceded him. Let him reflect that fashion is no bondage imposed by alien hands, but the last wisdom of his own kind, and that true dandyism is the result of an artistic temperament working upon a fine body within the wide limits of fashion. Through this habit of conformity, which it inculcates, the army has given us nearly all our finest dandies, from Alcibiades to Colonel Br-b-z-n *de nos jours*. Even Mr. Brummell, though he defied his Colonel, must have owed some of his success to the military spirit. Any parent intending his son to be a dandy will do well to send him first into the army, there to learn humility, as did his archetype, Apollo, in the house of Admetus. A sojourn at one of the Public Schools is also to be commended. The University it were well to avoid.

Of course, the dandy, like any other artist, has moments when his own period, palling, inclines him to antique modes. A fellow-student once told me that, after a long vacation spent in touch with modern life, he had hammered at the little gate of Merton and felt of a sudden his hat assume plumes and an expansive curl, the impress of a ruff about his neck, the dangle of a cloak and a sword. I, too, have my Elizabethan, my Caroline moments. I have gone to bed Georgian and awoken Early Victorian. Even savagery has charmed me. And at such times I have often wished I could find in my wardrobe suitable costumes. But these modish regrets are sterile, after all, and comprimend. What boots it to defy the conventions of our time? The dandy is the “child of his age,” and his best work must be produced in accord with the age’s natural influence. The true dandy must always love contemporary costume. In this age, as in all precedent ages, it is only the tasteless who cavil, being impotent to win from it fair results. How futile their voices are! The costume of the nineteenth century, as shadowed for us first by Mr. Brummell, so quiet, so reasonable, and, I say emphatically, so beautiful; free from folly or affectation, yet susceptible to exquisite ordering; plastic, austere, economical, may not be ignored. I spoke of the doom of swift rebellions, but I doubt even if any soever gradual evolution will lead us astray from the general precepts of Mr. Brummell’s code. At every step in the progress of democracy those precepts will be strengthened. Every day their fashion is more secure, corroborate. They are acknowledged by the world. The barbarous costumes that in bygone days were designed by class-hatred, or hatred of race, are dying, very surely dying. The costermonger with his pearl-emblazoned coat has been driven even from that Variety Stage, whereon he sought a desperate sanctuary. The clinquant corslet of the Swiss girl just survives at *bals costumés*. I am told that the kilt is now confined entirely to certain of the soldiery and to a small cult of Scotch Archaïcists. I have seen men flock from the boulevards of one capital and from the avenues of another to be clad in Conduit Street. Even into Oxford, that curious little city, where nothing is ever born nor anything ever quite dies, the force of the movement has penetrated, insomuch that tasselled cap and gown of degree are rarely seen in the streets or colleges. In a place which was until recent times scarcely less remote, Japan, the white and scarlet gardens are trod by men who are shod in boots like our own, who walk﻿—rather strangely still﻿—in close-cut cloth of little colour, and stop each other from time to time, laughing to show how that they too can furl an umbrella after the manner of real Europeans.

It is very nice, this universal acquiescence in the dress we have designed, but, if we reflect, not wonderful. There are three apparent reasons, and one of them is aesthetic. So to clothe the body that its fineness be revealed and its meanness veiled has been the aesthetic aim of all costume, but before our time the mean had never been struck. The ancient Romans went too far. Muffled in the ponderous folds of a toga, Adonis might pass for Punchinello, Punchinello for Adonis. The ancient Britons, on the other hand, did not go far enough. And so it had been in all ages down to that bright morning when Mr. Brummell, at his mirror, conceived the notion of trousers and simple coats. Clad according to his convention, the limbs of the weakling escape contempt, and the athlete is unobtrusive, and all is well. But there is also a social reason for the triumph of our costume﻿—the reason of economy. That austerity, which has rejected from its toilet silk and velvet and all but a few jewels, has made more ample the wardrobes of Dives, and sent forth Irus nicely dressed among his fellows. And lastly there is a reason of psychology, most potent of all, perhaps. Is not the costume of today, with its subtlety and sombre restraint, its quiet congruities of black and white and grey, supremely apt a medium for the expression of modern emotion and modern thought? That aptness, even alone, would explain its triumph. Let us be glad that we have so easy, yet so delicate, a mode of expression.

===Chinese===

我常常想，这种自私的专注——它是纨绔作风的一部分——也是每一门艺术的从业者都在不同程度上感到的那种孤寂（einsamkeit）的象征。但奇怪得很，恰恰是纨绔子的心灵与他的工底材料融为一体这一点，使他暴露于世俗的影响之下。从某种意义上说，纨绔作风是诸门艺术中最不自私的一门。音乐家给人看得见，却除非付钱，听不见。诗人写下的东西，你也只有付了钱才能读。画家也并非都像沃茨先生（Mr. Watts）那样慷慨。而纨绔子每次冲出前门，便是把自己呈献给了整个国家。君王与农夫一样，都可以瞻仰他的杰作。而任何一门就在公众眼皮底下经营的艺术，总比公众只能隔一层过问的艺术远更受时髦的摆布。艺术家们已渐渐让公众习惯了的那些标准，公众是不会眼看人轻易作践的。譬如，剧院的传统就极其严苛。倘若我兄弟在干草市场剧院（Haymarket）里用麦克里迪（Macready）那种浮丽洪亮的腔调朗诵台词，楼座里准会闹翻了天！剧院的变迁，只能经由那无形的演进过程。同样，在衣装的领域里，任何迅疾的叛乱都休想得逞——亲王复兴及膝马裤（knee-breeches）的尝试便是明证。倘若殿下当年凭他的睿智选择穿那种扎在靴底下的紧身长裤，「紧腿裤」（smalls）说不定倒能轮到重现，而到头来——谁知道呢？——及膝马裤也会回来。时新的推进，只能靠这个或那个纨绔子添上或删去一点、放宽或收紧一分，再由其余众人仿效。年轻的纨绔子会发现若干他必须遵从的法则。他若糟践了它们，街上的顽童就会冲他起哄，而且并非冤枉他，因为他糟践的是先行艺术家们缓慢营造起来的法则。且让他想一想：时髦并非异邦之手强加的桎梏，而是他这一行人自己的最高智慧；真正的纨绔作风，乃是艺术气质在时髦的宽广限度之内、施加于一具美好身体的结果。正是军队以其教人遵从的习惯，给了我们几乎全部最出色的纨绔子——从亚西比德（Alcibiades）直到我们这个时代的布-巴-宗上校（Colonel Br-b-z-n，de nos jours）。连布鲁梅尔先生，尽管顶撞过他的上校，其成功也必有几分要归功于军人的精神。凡有意让儿子做纨绔子的父母，最好先送他进军营，在那里学一学谦卑——一如他的原型阿波罗（Apollo）当年在阿德墨托斯（Admetus）家中那样。在某一所公学里过一段时日，也值得推荐。大学则最好避开。

当然，纨绔子同任何别的艺术家一样，也有这样的时刻：他自己的时代叫他生腻，引他倾心于古式的衣装。一位同学有一次告诉我：过了一个与现代生活朝夕相亲的长假之后，他叩响了默顿学院（Merton）的小门，忽然觉得自己的帽子上生出了羽翎和翻张的卷边，颈间压出了拉夫领的印痕，肩后晃荡起大氅和剑。我也有我的伊丽莎白时代的时刻、我的查理王时代的时刻。我曾顶着乔治王朝的排场上床，一觉醒来变成了维多利亚早期的人。连野蛮之风也曾令我心醉。每逢这种时候，我常常希望能从衣橱里翻出合式的行头来。但这些慕古的怅惘，终究是贫瘠的、徒惹人笑的。违抗我们时代的成规，究竟有什么益处？纨绔子是「时代的儿子」，他的最佳作品必须顺应时代的自然影响而产生。真正的纨绔子必须永远爱当代的衣装。在这个时代，一如在以往一切时代，只有没有趣味的人才对它百般挑剔，因为他们无力从中赢得美好的结果。他们的声音多么徒劳！十九世纪的衣装——最先由布鲁梅尔先生为我们投下影子的——那样安静，那样通情达理，而且，我毫不含糊地说，那样美丽；既无痴愚，亦无矫饰，却又经得起精微的调度；可塑、严正、俭省——是不容忽视的。迅疾叛乱的厄运我说过了，但我怀疑，哪怕再怎样渐进的演进，能不能把我们引离布鲁梅尔先生法典的总纲。民主每前进一步，那些准则便增强一分。它们的流行日甚一日地稳固、确凿。它们已为全世界所公认。往昔那些由阶级仇恨或种族仇恨设计出来的野蛮衣装，正在死去，千真万确地死去。那位外套上缀满珍珠纹章的水果小贩，已被赶得连杂耍戏台都待不住了——他原想在那台上寻个绝望的庇护所。瑞士姑娘那金光灿灿的紧身胸衣，只在化装舞会上聊存一息。我听说苏格兰褶裙如今已完全局限于一部分军人和一小撮苏格兰古风会信徒。我见过男人们从这座都城的林荫大道、从那座都城的大街，成群结队涌到康迪特街去置办行头。连牛津那座古怪的小城——在那里，无物诞生，也无物真正死去——这股运动的势力也已渗透进去，以致流苏方帽和学位袍在街衢学院之间已属罕见。在一个直到晚近还同样偏远的地方——日本——那白色与猩红相间的庭园里，如今走动着脚登与我们相仿的靴子的男人，他们身穿——说来仍旧有点古怪——裁得贴身、色彩淡素的呢料，时时停下彼此招呼，笑着表明他们也能照真正欧洲人的样子收拢一把伞。

真是令人愉快啊，世人对我们设计的服装这样普遍地默从——不过细想之下，并不奇怪。显见的理由有三，其中之一是审美的。使身体这样着装：精美处得以显露，寒碜处得以遮掩——这向来是一切衣装的审美鹄的；但在我们之前，这中道从未被命中。古罗马人走过头了。裹进托加袍沉重的褶层里，阿多尼斯（Adonis）会被错当成潘奇内洛（Punchinello），潘奇内洛被错当成阿多尼斯。古不列颠人呢，又走得不够远。历朝历代，莫不如此，直到那个晴明的早晨——布鲁梅尔先生对着镜子，构想出长裤和简朴的上衣。照他的成规着装，孱弱者的四肢免于轻蔑，矫健者不事张扬，万事妥帖。但我们衣装的胜利，还有一个社会的理由——经济的理由。那份把丝绸、天鹅绒和一切珠宝（除寥寥几件）都逐出梳妆的严正，既使财主（Dives）的衣橱愈见充盈，又让伊鲁斯（Irus）衣冠楚楚地回到他的同伴中间去。最后还有一个心理学的理由，或许是一切理由中最有力的。今日的衣装，凭它的精微与暗色的节制，凭它黑白灰的静默谐调，岂不是表达现代情绪与现代思想的一件再妥帖不过的媒介？单凭这份妥帖，便足以解释它的胜利了。且让我们庆幸：我们竟有这样一种既轻易又精妙的表达方式。

===Original===

Yes! costume, dandiacal or not, is in the highest degree expressive, nor is there any type it may not express. It enables us to classify any “professional man” at a glance, be he lawyer, leech or whatnot. Still more swift and obvious is its revelation of the work and the soul of those who dress, whether naturally or for effect, without reference to convention. The bowler of Mr. Jerome K. Jerome is a perfect preface to all his works. The silk hat of Mr. Whistler is a real *nocturne*, his linen a symphony *en blanc majeur*. To have seen Mr. Hall Caine is to have read his soul. His flowing, formless cloak is as one of his own novels, twenty-five editions latent in the folds of it. Melodrama crouches upon the brim of his sombrero. His tie is a Publisher’s Announcement. His boots are Copyright. In his hand he holds the staff of *The Family Herald*.

But the dandy, in no wise violating the laws of fashion, can make more subtle symbols of his personality. More subtle these symbols are for the very reason that they are effected within the restrictions which are essential to an art. Chastened of all flamboyance, they are from most men occult, obvious, it may be, only to other artists or even only to him they symbolise. Nor will the dandy express merely a crude idea of his personality, as does, for example, Mr. Hall Caine, dressing himself always and exactly after one pattern. Every day as his mood has changed since his last toilet, he will vary the colour, texture, form of his costume. Fashion does not rob him of free will. It leaves him liberty of all expression. Every day there is not one accessory, from the butterfly that alights above his shirt front to the jewels planted in his linen, that will not symbolise the mood that is in him or the occasion of the coming day.

On this, the psychological side of foppery, I know not one so expert as him whom, not greatly caring for contemporary names, I will call Mr. Le V. No hero-worshipper am I, but I cannot write without enthusiasm of his simple life. He has not spurred his mind to the quest of shadows nor vexed his soul in the worship of any gods. No woman has wounded his heart, though he has gazed gallantly into the eyes of many women, intent, I fancy, upon his own miniature there. Nor is the incomparable set of his trousers spoilt by the perching of any dear little child upon his knee. And so, now that he is stricken with seventy years, he knows none of the bitterness of eld, for his toilet-table is an imperishable altar, his wardrobe a quiet nursery and very constant harem. Mr. Le V. has many disciples, young men who look to him for guidance in all that concerns costume, and each morning come, themselves tentatively clad, to watch the perfect procedure of his toilet and learn invaluable lessons. I myself, a lie-a-bed, often steal out, foregoing the best hours of the day abed, that I may attend that levée. The rooms of the Master are in St. James’s Street, and perhaps it were well that I should give some little record of them and of the manner of their use. In the first room the Master sleeps. He is called by one of his valets, at seven o’clock, to the second room, where he bathes, is shampooed, is manicured and, at length, is enveloped in a dressing-gown of white wool. In the third room is his breakfast upon a little table and his letters and some newspapers. Leisurely he sips his chocolate, leisurely learns all that need be known. With a cigarette he allows his temper, as informed by the news and the weather and whatnot, to develop itself for the day. At length, his mood suggests, imperceptibly, what colour, what form of clothes he shall wear. He rings for his valet﻿—“I will wear such and such a coat, such and such a tie; my trousers shall be of this or that tone; this or that jewel shall be radiant in the folds of my tie.” It is generally near noon that he reaches the fourth room, the dressing-room. The uninitiate can hardly realise how impressive is the ceremonial there enacted. As I write, I can see, in memory, the whole scene﻿—the room, severely simple, with its lemon walls and deep wardrobes of white wood, the young fops, *φιλομαθέστατοι τιυες τῶν νεανίσκων*, ranged upon a long bench, rapt in wonder, and, in the middle, now sitting, now standing, negligently, before a long mirror, with a valet at either elbow, Mr. Le V., our cynosure. There is no haste, no faltering, when once the scheme of the day’s toilet has been set. It is a calm toilet. A flower does not grow more calmly.

===Chinese===

是的！衣装，无论纨绔与否，都是最高度地富于表现力的，也没有任何类型是它表现不了的。它使我们一望之间便能把任何一位「专业人士」归了类，不管他是律师、水蛭（旧时对医生的谑称）还是别的什么。至于那些置成规于不顾而着装的人——无论出于天性还是为了效果——衣装对他们行当与灵魂的揭示，还要更迅捷、更显豁。杰罗姆·K·杰罗姆先生（Mr. Jerome K. Jerome）那顶圆顶硬帽，是他全部著述的一篇完满的序言。惠斯勒先生的大礼帽是一幅真正的「夜曲」，他的亚麻衣衫是一首「白色大调」的交响曲（symphonie en blanc majeur）。见过霍尔·凯恩先生（Mr. Hall Caine），就等于读过了他的灵魂。他那件飘垂无形的大氅，恰似他自己的一部小说，褶层里潜伏着二十五版印数。情节剧蹲踞在他阔边帽的帽檐上。他的领带是一则出版预告。他的靴子是版权所有。他手里握着的那根杖，便是《家庭先驱报》（The Family Herald）的「社杖」。

而纨绔子，在丝毫不违犯时髦法则的前提下，能把自己的人格造成更精微的象征。这些象征之所以更精微，正因为它们是在一门艺术必不可少的限制之内造成的。涤尽了全部浮艳之后，它们在大多数人眼里是隐秘的，或许只对其他艺术家显豁，甚至只对它们所象征的那个人自己显豁。纨绔子也不会只表达一种粗糙的人格观念，比如霍尔·凯恩先生那样，永远分毫不差地按同一个款式穿戴。自从上一次梳妆以来，他的心情日日不同，他也将随之变换衣装的颜色、质地与形制。时髦并不剥夺他的自由意志。它留给他表达一切的自由。每一天，从栖落在衬衫前襟上方的那只蝴蝶，到栽植在他亚麻衣衫里的珠宝，没有一件配饰不象征着他此刻的情怀，或未来一天的场合。

在纨绔作风的这一心理侧面，我不知道有谁比那位行家更高明——我不大爱提当代人的名姓，姑且称他勒·V先生（Mr. Le V.）。我不是个崇拜英雄的人，可是一写起他简朴的生活，我便无法不怀着热诚。他不曾驱策心智去追逐幻影，也不曾为崇拜任何神明而折磨灵魂。没有一个女人伤过他的心，虽然他曾倜傥地凝望过许多女人的眼睛——我猜想，是专注于自己映在其中的小小画像。他那无可匹敌的裤线，也从不曾被哪个可爱的小孩坐上膝头而压坏。于是，如今他已届七十高龄，却全然不知老来的苦涩，因为他的梳妆台是一座不朽的祭坛，他的衣橱是一间安静的育儿室，也是一个极坚贞的后宫。勒·V先生门徒众多，都是些年轻人，凡属衣装之事无不仰赖他的指点，每天清晨穿得将信将疑地前来，观摩他梳妆的完美程序，领受无价的教益。我自己，一个贪恋床褥的人，也常常舍弃床上一天中最美好的时辰，偷偷溜出去，为的是赶上那场晨间觐见（levée）。大师的房间在圣詹姆斯街上，把那些房间连同它们的用法略记一笔，或许不无好处。在第一个房间里，大师就寝。七点钟，一名男仆唤醒他，请进第二个房间，他在那里沐浴、按摩头皮、修剪指甲，末了裹上一件白色羊毛晨衣。第三个房间里，一张小桌上摆着他的早餐、他的信件和几份报纸。他从容地啜着巧克力，从容地获知一切须要知道的事。燃起一支香烟，他听任自己的脾气——已被新闻、天气等等诸如此类的事物喂养成型的脾气——为这一天发育完全。终于，他的情绪在不知不觉间提示他：该穿什么颜色、什么式样的衣裳了。他摇铃唤来男仆——「我要穿这样一件上衣、这样一条领带；我的裤子要这种或那种色调；这颗或那颗珠宝，要让它在我的领带褶间放出光来。」一般总要临近正午，他才走进第四个房间——那间更衣室。未入此门的人，很难想见那里演行的仪典是何等气象。此刻下笔，我还能在记忆里看见整个场景——那房间俭素之至，柠檬色的墙壁，白木的深大立柜；年轻的纨绔子们——青年中最勤学好问的一群（φιλομαθέστατοι τιυες τῶν νεανίσκων）——在一条长凳上依次坐定，出神叹赏；而在正中，长镜之前，时而坐着、时而懒懒立着，左右肘边各侍立一名男仆的，便是勒·V先生，我们众目所归的北极星。当日的梳妆方案一经敲定，便再无匆促，再无疑滞。那是一场安静的梳妆。一朵花开得也没有这样安静。

===Original===

Any of us, any day, may see the gracious figure of Mr. Le V., as he saunters down the slope of St. James’s. Long may the sun irradiate the surface of his tilted hat! It is comfortable to know that, though he die tomorrow, the world will not lack a most elaborate record of his foppery. All his life he has kept or, rather, the current valets have kept for him, a *Journal de Toilette*. Of this there are now fifty volumes, each covering the space of a year. Yes, fifty springs have filled his buttonhole with their violets; the snow of fifty winters has been less white than his linen; his boots have outshone fifty sequences of summer suns, and the colours of all those autumns have faded in the dry light of his apparel. The first page of each volume of the *Journal de Toilette* bears the signature of Mr. Le V. and of his two valets. Of the other pages each is given up, as in other diaries, to one day of the year. In ruled spaces are recorded there the cut and texture of the suit, the colour of the tie, the form of jewellery that was worn on the day the page records. No detail is omitted and a separate space is set aside for “Remarks.” I remember that I once asked Mr. Le V., half in jest, what he should wear on the Judgment Day. Seriously, and (I fancied) with a note of pathos in his voice, he said to me, “Young man, you ask me to lay bare my soul to you. If I had been a saint I should certainly wear a light suit, with a white waistcoat and a flower, but I am no saint, sir, no saint.﻿ ﻿… I shall probably wear black trousers or trousers of some very dark blue, and a frock-coat, tightly buttoned.” Poor old Mr. Le V.! I think he need not fear. If there be a heaven for the soul, there must be other heavens also, where the intellect and the body shall be consummate. In both these heavens Mr. Le V. will have his hierarchy. Of a life like his there can be no conclusion, really. Did not even Matthew Arnold admit that conduct of a cane is three-fourths of life?

Certainly Mr. Le V. is a great artist, and his supremacy is in the tact with which he suits his toilet to his temperament. But the marvellous affinity of a dandy’s mood to his daily toilet is not merely that it finds therein its perfect echo nor that it may even be, in reflex, thereby accentuated or made less poignant. For some years I had felt convinced that in a perfect dandy this affinity must reach a point, when the costume itself, planned with the finest sensibility, would change with the emotional changes of its wearer, automatically. But I felt that here was one of those boundaries, where the fields of art align with the fields of science, and I hardly dared to venture further. Moreover, the theory was not easy to verify. I knew that, except in some great emotional crisis, the costume could not palpably change its aspect. Here was an impasse; for the perfect dandy﻿—the Brummell, the Mr. Le V.﻿—cannot afford to indulge in any great emotion outside his art; like Balzac, he has not time. The gods were good to me, however. One morning near the end of last July, they decreed that I should pass through Half Moon Street and meet there a friend who should ask me to go with him to his club and watch for the results of the racing at Goodwood. This club includes hardly any member who is not a devotee of the Turf, so that, when we entered it, the cloakroom displayed long rows of unburdened pegs﻿—save where one hat shone. None but that illustrious dandy, Lord X., wears quite so broad a brim as this hat had. I said that Lord X. must be in the club.

===Chinese===

我们任何人，随便哪一天，都可能望见勒·V先生那雍容的身影，正沿圣詹姆斯的坡道信步而下。愿太阳长久照耀他微微倾侧的帽面！想到纵使他明天故去，世人也不致缺少一份关于他纨绔行状的最详尽的记载，实在叫人心安。他一生都在记——或者不如说，是历届男仆替他记着——一部《梳妆日志》（Journal de Toilette）。如今已有五十卷，每卷记满一年。是啊，五十个春天曾以紫罗兰插满他的纽孔；五十个冬天的积雪，也不及他的亚麻衣衫那样白；他的靴子曾让五十轮夏天的太阳黯然失色，而所有那些秋天的色彩，都在他衣装的干爽光辉里褪尽了。每卷《梳妆日志》的第一页，都有勒·V先生和他的两名男仆的签名。其余各页，同别的日记一样，每页记一年中的一天。一格格横线栏里记着当日全套衣装的剪裁与质地、领带的颜色、所佩珠宝的形制——即该页所记那一天穿戴的。无一处细节遗漏，还单辟一栏作「备注」。我记得有一回，我半开玩笑地问勒·V先生，审判日那天他打算穿什么。他一本正经地——我觉得嗓音里还带着一丝悲怆——对我说：「年轻人，你这是要我向你袒露灵魂。我要是个圣徒，我准会穿一套浅色衣裳，配一件白马甲，戴一朵花；可我不是圣徒，先生，不是圣徒。……我大概会穿黑裤子，或者非常深的蓝裤子，再穿一件纽扣扣得紧紧的长礼服。」可怜的勒·V老先生！我想他不必担心。倘若灵魂有天堂，那就必定还有别的天堂，在那里智性与肉身各得圆满。在这两重天堂里，勒·V先生都将有他的品级。像他那样的生活，实在没有终结可言。马修·阿诺德（Matthew Arnold）不也承认过，手杖的举止乃人生之四分之三么？

勒·V先生当然是一位大艺术家，他的至高之处，在于以那样妥帖的分寸使梳妆契于气质。然而，纨绔子的情绪与他每日梳妆之间那种奇妙的契合，并不仅仅在于情绪在其中找到完美的回声，也不仅仅在于它甚至可能反过来被梳妆渲染得更浓或冲淡几分。多年来我一直相信：在一个完美的纨绔子身上，这种契合必会达到这样一点——那以最精微的感受力设计出来的衣装本身，将随着穿衣者情绪的起伏而自动变化。但我觉得，这里是那些疆界之一：艺术的田畴与科学的田畴在此接壤，我几乎不敢再往前闯。何况这理论也不容易验证。我知道，除非碰上某种剧烈的情绪危机，衣装是不会明显改换面貌的。这就成了一条绝路；因为完美的纨绔子——布鲁梅尔们、勒·V先生们——可消受不起在他的艺术之外纵情于任何剧烈的情绪；他像巴尔扎克一样，没有那个工夫。然而诸神待我不薄。去年七月末的一个早晨，他们注定我该穿过半月街（Half Moon Street），在那里遇见一位朋友，他邀我同去他的俱乐部，守候古德伍德赛马的结果。这家俱乐部里，几乎没有一个会员不是赛马场的信徒，因此我们进去时，衣帽间里只见一长排一长排空荡荡的挂钩——唯有一处，一顶帽子熠熠生光。除了那位声名赫赫的纨绔子X勋爵（Lord X.），没有别人戴得出这么宽的帽檐。我说，X勋爵必定就在俱乐部里。

===Original===

“I conceive he is too nervous to be on the course,” my friend replied. “They say he has plunged up to the hilt on today’s running.”

His lordship was indeed there, fingering feverishly the sinuous ribands of the tape-machine. I sat at a little distance, watching him. Two results straggled forth within an hour, and, at the second of these, I saw with wonder Lord X.’s linen actually flush for a moment and then turn deadly pale. I looked again and saw that his boots had lost their lustre. Drawing nearer, I found that grey hairs had begun to show themselves in his raven coat. It was very painful and yet, to me, very gratifying. In the cloakroom, when I went for my own hat and cane, there was the hat with the broad brim, and (lo!) over its iron-blue surface little furrows had been ploughed by Despair.

Rouen, 1896.

===Chinese===

「我看他是紧张得不敢亲临赛场了，」我的朋友答道。「听说今天这一场，他把注押到了刀柄。」

勋爵大人果然在那里，手指狂乱地捻弄着行情纸带机蜿蜒吐出的纸带。我坐在不远处观望。一个钟头之内，迟迟疑疑地蹦出了两个赛果；就在第二个赛果出来时，我惊奇地看见X勋爵的亚麻衣衫当真红了一瞬，随即转为死一般的苍白。再看时，他的靴子已经失去了光泽。凑近一些，我又发现他那乌黑的礼服上已开始露出几茎灰发。这一幕叫人很是痛心，然而于我，又很是可心。后来我去衣帽间取自己的帽子和手杖，那顶宽檐帽还在；看哪！在它铁蓝色的帽面上，「绝望」已经犁出了一道道细细的沟。

鲁昂，1896 年。

## Diminuendo / 渐弱

===Original===

In the year of grace 1890, and in the beautiful autumn of that year, I was a freshman at Oxford. I remember how my tutor asked me what lectures I wished to attend, and how he laughed when I said that I wished to attend the lectures of Mr. Walter Pater. Also I remember how, one morning soon after, I went into Ryman’s to order some foolish engraving for my room, and there saw, peering into a portfolio, a small, thick, rock-faced man, whose top-hat and gloves of *bright* dog-skin struck one of the many discords in that little city of learning or laughter. The serried bristles of his moustachio made for him a false-military air. I think I nearly went down when they told me that this was Pater.

Not that even in those more decadent days of my childhood did I admire the man as a stylist. Even then I was angry that he should treat English as a dead language, bored by that sedulous ritual wherewith he laid out every sentence as in a shroud﻿—hanging, like a widower, long over its marmoreal beauty or ever he could lay it at length in his book, its sepulchre. From that laden air, the so cadaverous murmur of that sanctuary, I would hook it at the beck of any jade. The writing of Pater had never, indeed, appealed to me, *άλλ’ αἰεί*, having regard to the couth solemnity of his mind, to his philosophy, his rare erudition, *τινα φῶτα μέγαν καὶ καλὸν ἐδέγμην*.8 And I suppose it was when at length I saw him that I first knew him to be fallible.

At school I had read *Marius the Epicurean* in bed and with a dark lantern. Indeed, I regarded it mainly as a tale of adventure, quite as fascinating as *Midshipman Easy*, and far less hard to understand, because there were no nautical terms in it. Marryat, moreover, never made me wish to run away to sea, whilst certainly Pater did make me wish for more “colour” in the curriculum, for a renaissance of the Farrar period, when there was always “a sullen spirit of revolt against the authorities”; when lockers were always being broken into and marks falsified, and small boys prevented from saying their prayers, insomuch that they vowed they would no longer buy brandy for their seniors. In some schools, I am told, the pretty old custom of roasting a fourth-form boy, whole, upon Founder’s Day still survives. But in my school there was less sentiment. I ended by acquiescing in the slow revolution of its wheel of work and play. I felt that at Oxford, when I should be of age to matriculate, a “variegated dramatic life” was waiting for me. I was not a little too sanguine, alas!

===Chinese===

公元 1890 年，正当那年的美丽秋日，我在牛津做了一名新生。我记得导师问我想听哪些先生的课，而当我说想听沃尔特·佩特（Walter Pater）先生的课时，他是怎样发笑。我也记得此后不久的一个早晨，我走进莱曼铺子（Ryman's），要为我的房间订一幅无聊的雕版画，却在那里看见一个矮墩墩、厚实实、一脸岩石的汉子，正俯身细看一册画夹；他的大礼帽，还有他那双*雪亮的*狗皮手套，恰在这座学问抑或笑闹的小城里，奏出了诸多不谐和音中的一记。他唇上髭须硬毛森然，替他平添了一副冒牌的军人气概。我想，当他们告诉我这位便是佩特的时候，我几乎当场拜倒了。

倒不是说我早在少年时代那些更为颓唐的日子里，便敬服此人的文体。即便在当时，我已恼怒于他竟把英语当作一门死语言来侍弄，也厌倦于他敷演每一个句子的那套殷勤仪轨——他把句子如殓尸般装裹起来，又像鳏夫似的，对着它大理石般的美久久俯身流连，迟迟才终予把它安放进他的书里，那句子的墓穴。从那凝重的空气里、从那圣所如此尸气沉沉的喃喃低语中，只消随便哪个轻佻女人一招手，我便溜之大吉。诚然，佩特的文字于我从来不曾有过吸引力，*άλλ’ αἰεί*（然而我始终……），但念及他心智的温雅庄重、他的哲思、他罕觏的渊博，*τινα φῶτα μέγαν καὶ καλὸν ἐδέγμην*.8（我总把他当作一位伟大而美好的人物来敬迎）。

在学校里，我是躺在床上、就着一盏遮光灯读《享乐主义者马里乌斯》（*Marius the Epicurean*）的。说实在的，我大体把它当作一部冒险故事，与《海军候补生伊齐》（*Midshipman Easy*）同样引人入胜，而且好懂得多，因为里头没有航海术语。何况马里亚特（Marryat）从不曾引得我想私逃出海，佩特却当真引得我盼着课程表里多些「色彩」，盼着法拉尔（Farrar）时期的复兴——那时节总有「一股郁郁反抗师长之风」；总有人撬开储物柜、涂改分数，总有幼小的同学被搅得做不成祷告，以致他们赌咒发誓，再不给高年级学长买白兰地了。听说在有些学校，建校纪念日把一名四年级学童囫囵架起来烤的妙古风俗犹存。但在我的学校里，这番情致要少些。到头来，我只得默许功课与游戏之轮的缓慢周转。我觉得，待我到了入学的年纪，牛津正有一场「五光十色的戏剧性生活」等着我。唉，我可未免太乐观了！


===Original===

How sad was my coming to the university! Where were those sweet conditions I had pictured in my boyhood? Those antique contrasts? Did I ride, one sunset, through fens on a palfrey, watching the gold reflections on Magdalen Tower? Did I ride over Magdalen Bridge and hear the consonance of evening-bells and cries from the river below? Did I rein in to wonder at the raised gates of Queen’s, the twisted pillars of St. Mary’s, the little shops, lighted with tapers? Did bull-pups snarl at me, or dons, with bent backs, acknowledge my salute? Anyone who knows the place as it is, must see that such questions are purely rhetorical. To him I need not explain the disappointment that beset me when, after being whirled in a cab from the station to a big hotel, I wandered out into the streets. *On aurait dit* a bit of Manchester through which Apollo had once passed; for here, among the hideous trains and the brand-new bricks﻿—here, glared at by the electric-lights that hung from poles, screamed at by boys with the *Echo* and the *Star*﻿—here, in a riot of vulgarity, were remnants of beauty, as I discerned. There were only remnants.

Soon also I found that the life of the place, like the place, had lost its charm and its tradition. Gone were the contrasts that made it wonderful. That feud between undergraduates and dons﻿—latent, in the old days, only at times when it behoved the two academic grades to unite against the townspeople﻿—was one of the absurdities of the past. The townspeople now looked just like undergraduates and the dons just like townspeople. So splendid was the train-service between Oxford and London that, with hundreds of passengers daily, the one had become little better than a suburb of the other. What more could extensionists demand? As for me, I was disheartened. Bitter were the comparisons I drew between my coming to Oxford and the coming of Marius to Rome. Could it be that there was at length no beautiful environment wherein a man might sound the harmonies of his soul? Had civilisation made beauty, besides adventure, so rare? I wondered what counsel Pater, insistent always upon contact with comely things, would offer to one who could nowhere find them. I had been wondering that very day when I went into Ryman’s and saw him there.

===Chinese===

我初到大学，何其黯然！少年时代悬想的那一幅幅甜美光景，都到哪里去了？那些古雅的对照呢？我可曾在某个日暮时分，骑一匹小马穿行泽地，望莫德林塔（Magdalen Tower）上流金泛彩？我可曾驰过莫德林桥（Magdalen Bridge），听晚钟与桥下河上的呼喊相和？我可曾勒住马，惊叹王后学院（Queen's）高轩的门楼、圣玛丽教堂（St. Mary's）螺旋的石柱、那些点着细蜡烛的小铺面？可有小叭狗朝我狺狺，可有弯腰弓背的学监答我的礼？凡识得此地今日真面目的人，必看得出这些问话纯属修辞。用不着我向他解释：从车站被一辆马车卷到大旅馆之后，我踱出门去逛街时，那失望如何四面围拢。*On aurait dit*（简直可以说）是曼彻斯特（Manchester）的一角，只是阿波罗曾一度降临；因为在这儿，在丑陋的火车与簇新的砖块之间——在这儿，杆上悬着的电灯瞪视着你，卖《回声报》（*Echo*）和《星报》（*Star*）的报童朝你尖叫——在这儿，一片俗丽的喧嚣之中，犹存着美的残迹，如我所辨。只有残迹而已。

不久我又发觉，此地的生涯，一如此地本身，也失了它的魅力与它的传统。那些使它显得奇妙的对照，一去不返了。本科生与学监之间的宿仇——往日里，惟当两个学术阶层理应联手对付市民时方才潜藏——已属往昔的荒唐事。如今市民看上去恰似本科生，学监看上去恰似市民。牛津与伦敦之间的火车班次如此完善，每日往来旅客成百，前者简直成了后者的一座郊区。大学推广运动的诸君还能别有何求？至于我，则意气消沉。我拿自己之来牛津与马里乌斯之来罗马两相并举，何其酸楚。莫非当真再没有一处美的环境，容人谛听自己灵魂的和声了么？文明莫非把美——连同奇遇一并——弄得这般稀罕了么？我纳罕，佩特那位一味坚执须与美好事物相亲的人，对一个遍地觅不得美好事物的人，能开出什么方子。那一天我正为这节纳罕，便走进莱曼铺子，看见了他。


===Original===

When the tumult of my disillusioning was past, my mind grew clearer. I discerned that the scope of my quest for emotion must be narrowed. That abandonment of one’s self to life, that merging of one’s soul in bright waters, so often suggested in Pater’s writing, were a counsel impossible for today. The quest of emotions must be no less keen, certainly, but the manner of it must be changed forthwith. To unswitch myself from my surroundings, to guard my soul from contact with the unlovely things that compassed it about, therein lay my hope. I must approach the Benign Mother with great caution. And so, while most of the freshmen were doing her honour with wine and song and wreaths of smoke, I stood aside, pondered. In such seclusion I passed my first term﻿—ah, how often did I wonder whether I was not wasting my days, and, wondering, abandon my meditations upon the right ordering of the future! Thanks be to Athene, who threw her shadow over me in those moments of weak folly!

At the end of term I came to London. Around me seethed swirls, eddies, torrents, violent crosscurrents of human activity. What uproar! Surely I could have no part in modern life. Yet, yet for a while it was fascinating to watch the ways of its children. The prodigious life of the Prince of Wales fascinated me above all; indeed, it still fascinates me. What experience has been withheld from His Royal Highness? Was ever so supernal a type, as he, of mere Pleasure? How often he has watched, at Newmarket, the scud-a-run of quivering homuncules over the vert on horses, or, from some night-boat, the holocaust of great wharves by the side of the Thames; raced through the blue Solent; threaded *les coulisses*! He has danced in every palace of every capital, played in every club. He has hunted eleplants through the jungles of India, boar through the forests of Austria, pigs over the plains of Massachusetts. From the Castle of Abergeldie he has led his Princess into the frosty night, Highlanders lighting with torches the path to the deer-larder, where lay the wild things that had fallen to him on the crags. He has marched the Grenadiers to chapel through the white streets of Windsor. He has ridden through Moscow, in strange apparel, to kiss the catafalque of more than one Tzar. For him the Rajahs of India have spoiled their temples, and Blondin has crossed Niagara along the tightrope, and the Giant Guard done drill beneath the chandeliers of the Neue Schloss. Incline he to scandal, lawyers are proud to whisper their secrets in his ear. Be he gallant, the ladies are at his feet. Ennuyé, all the wits from Bernal Osborne to Arthur Roberts have jested for him. He has been “present always at the focus where the greatest number of forces unite in their purest energy,” for it is his presence that makes those forces unite.

===Chinese===

幻灭的喧嚣过后，我的心智澄澈起来。我看出，我对情感的追索，范围必须收窄。那种把自我委弃给生活、把灵魂融汇于明波之中的做法——佩特笔下屡屡暗示的那般——在今日已是不可能奉行的箴言。对情感的追索，固然还须一样地热切，但其方式必须即刻改弦更张。把自己从周遭的环境里摘开，守住灵魂，不叫它与四面团围的不美之物相接——我的希望正在于此。我必得以十二分的审慎，去亲近这位慈蔼的母亲。于是，当多数新生正以美酒、歌声和烟圈儿结成的花环向她致敬时，我退在一旁，沉思。我在这样的幽独中度过第一学期——啊，我曾多少次疑心自己是在虚掷光阴，又多少次一边疑心，一边丢开了对将来如何善自安排的思索！感谢雅典娜（Athene），是她在那些软弱愚妄的时刻，把她的影子覆在了我身上！

学期一结束，我便到了伦敦。我四周沸涌着人类活动的漩涡、涡流、急湍与剧烈的交叉水流。何等的喧嚣！现代生活于我自然是无缘的。然而，然而有一阵子，冷眼看它那些儿女的行止，倒也迷人。威尔士亲王那非凡绝伦的生活，尤令我着迷；说真的，至今仍令我着迷。殿下还有什么阅历不曾享过？单论「快乐」，古往今来可曾有过像他这般超凡入圣的化身？在纽马基特（Newmarket），他曾多少回观看那些战栗的小人儿伏在马背上、掠着草绿疾飞；从某艘夜航船上，他曾多少回观看泰晤士（Thames）河畔的大码头葬身烈焰；他曾在蓝色的索伦特海峡（Solent）里破浪竞渡；曾穿行于*les coulisses*（剧院后台）之间！每座京城的每座宫殿里，他都跳过舞；每家俱乐部里，他都玩过牌。他曾在印度的丛林里猎象，在奥地利的森林里猎野猪，在马萨诸塞（Massachusetts）的平原上猎猪猡。从阿伯格尔迪城堡（Castle of Abergeldie），他曾携他的亲王妃步入霜夜，苏格兰高地人执火炬照亮通往鹿肉房的小径，那里陈着他从山岩间射获的野物。他曾在温莎（Windsor）雪白的街道上，率掷弹兵卫队（Grenadiers）赴礼拜堂。他曾在莫斯科（Moscow），身着奇装，骑马去吻不止一位沙皇（Tzar）的灵柩。为他，印度的拉甲们不惜糟蹋了自己的神庙；布朗丹（Blondin）沿着绷索渡过了尼亚加拉（Niagara）；巨人卫队在「新宫」（Neue Schloss）的枝形吊灯下操练。他若倾心于丑闻，律师们便荣幸地凑到他耳边低诉机密。他若是多情种，太太小姐们便拜倒在他脚下。他若是腻烦了，从伯纳尔·奥斯本（Bernal Osborne）到亚瑟·罗伯茨（Arthur Roberts）的所有才子，都曾为他凑趣逗笑。他一向「永远在场，居于最众多力量以其最纯粹能量相汇的焦点」，因为正是他的在场，才使那些力量得以相汇。


===Original===

“Ennuyé?” I asked. Indeed he never is. How could he be when Pleasure hangs constantly upon his arm! It is those others, overtaking her only after arduous chase, breathless and footsore, who quickly sicken of her company, and fall fainting at her feet. And for me, shod neither with rank nor riches, what folly to join the chase! I began to see how small a thing it were to sacrifice those external “experiences,” so dear to the heart of Pater, by a rigid, complex civilisation made so hard to gain. They gave nothing but lassitude to those who had gained them through suffering. Even to the kings and princes, who so easily gained them, what did they yield besides themselves? I do not suppose that, if we were invited to give authenticated instances of intelligence on the part of our royal pets, we could fill half a column of the *Spectator*. In fact, their lives are so full they have no time for thought, the highest energy of man. Now, it was to thought that *my* life should be dedicated. Action, apart from its absorption of time, would war otherwise against the pleasures of intellect, which, for me, meant mainly the pleasures of imagination. It is only (this is a platitude) the things one has not done, the faces or places one has not seen, or seen but darkly, that have charm. It is only mystery﻿—such mystery as besets the eyes of children﻿—that makes things superb. I thought of the voluptuaries I had known﻿—they seemed so sad, so ascetic almost, like poor pilgrims, raising their eyes never or ever gazing at the moon of tarnished endeavour. I thought of the round, insouciant faces of the monks at whose monastery I once broke bread, and how their eyes sparkled when they asked me of the France that lay around their walls. I thought, pardie, of the lurid verses written by young men who, in real life, know no haunt more lurid than a literary public-house. It was, for me, merely a problem how I could best avoid “sensations,” “pulsations,” and “exquisite moments” that were not purely intellectual. I would not attempt to combine both kinds, as Pater seemed to fancy a man might. I would make myself master of some small area of physical life, a life of quiet, monotonous simplicity, exempt from all outer disturbance. I would shield my body from the world that my mind might range over it, not hurt nor fettered. As yet, however, I was in my first year at Oxford. There were many reasons that I should stay there and take my degree, reasons that I did not combat. Indeed, I was content to wait for my life.

===Chinese===

「厌倦？」我要问了。他实在从不厌倦。「快乐」既永远挽着他的手臂，他怎会厌倦！倒是旁的那些人，唯有历经艰辛的追逐方能追上她，气喘吁吁、脚底磨破，很快便腻了她的陪伴，晕倒在她脚边。至于我，脚上既无爵位可垫、亦无富贵可靴，竟也要去凑这场追逐，何其愚妄！我渐悟，牺牲那些外在的「阅历」——佩特心中的至爱、那被严峻繁复的文明弄得如此难得的阅历——原是何等小的一桩事。历尽辛苦挣得它们的人，从它们那里只落得一身困乏。就连轻易挣得它们的君王亲王们，除其自身之外，它们又出产过什么？我想要是请我们举出那些王家宠儿有智力的确凿实例，只怕《旁观者》报（*Spectator*）的半栏也填不满。事实上，他们的生活太满了，满得没有时间思想——那可是人类最高的精力。而我，我的生命就该献给思想。行动，姑且不论它耗蚀光阴，还会在别处同智性的愉悦开战；而智性的愉悦，于我主要是想象的愉悦。唯有（这是句老生常谈）不曾做过的事、不曾见过或只见得依稀的面孔与地方，才有魅力。唯有神秘——那种笼罩儿童眼睛的神秘——才使事物超凡入圣。我想起我识得的那些声色之徒——他们显得那么悲哀，几乎可谓苦行，像可怜的朝圣者，从不抬眼，或抬眼也只凝望那轮锈黯的劳碌之月。我想起我曾在那座修道院里分食过面包的修士们，一张张圆圆的、无忧无虑的脸，想起他们问起围墙外头的法兰西时，眼睛怎样发亮。我还想起——天知道——那些年轻人写的血光淋漓的诗行，而他们在实际生活里，见识过的最血光的地方，不过是一家文人酒馆。于我，问题仅仅在于：如何最妥善地避开那些并非纯乎智性的「感觉」、「悸动」与「精妙的瞬间」。我不打算像佩特所以为人所能的那样，把两类兼收并蓄。我要为自己掌管一小片肉体生活的疆域，过一种安静、单调、纯素、不受一切外扰的生活。我要把身体同世界隔开，好让我的心智纵横驰骋于其上，不受伤害，也不受捆缚。然而当时，我尚在牛津一年级。有许多理由要我留在那里拿学位，那些理由我并不抗辩。说真的，我情愿静候我的生活。


===Original===

And now that I have made my adieux to the Benign Mother, I need wait no longer. I have been casting my eye over the suburbs of London. I have taken a most pleasant little villa in —﻿—﻿ham, and here I shall make my home. Here there is no traffic, no harvest. Those of the inhabitants who do anything go away each morning and do it elsewhere. Here no vital forces unite. Nothing happens here. The days and the months will pass by me, bringing their sure recurrence of quiet events. In the springtime I shall look out from my window and see the laburnum flowering in the little front garden. In summer cool syrups will come for me from the grocer’s shop. Autumn will make the boughs of my mountain-ash scarlet, and, later, the asbestos in my grate will put forth its blossoms of flame. The infrequent cart of Buszard or Mudie will pass my window at all seasons. Nor will this be all. I shall have friends. Next door, there is a retired military man who has offered, in a most neighbourly way, to lend me his copy of the *Times*. On the other side of my house lives a charming family, who perhaps will call on me, now and again. I have seen them sally forth, at sundown, to catch the theatre-train; among them walked a young lady, the charm of whose figure was ill concealed by the neat waterproof that overspread her evening dress. Some day it may be﻿ ﻿… but I anticipate. These things will be but the cosy accompaniment of my days. For I shall contemplate the world.

I shall look forth from my window, the laburnum and the mountain-ash becoming mere silhouettes in the foreground of my vision. I shall look forth and, in nay remoteness, appreciate the distant pageant of the world. Humanity will range itself in the columns of my morning paper. No pulse of life will escape me. The strife of politics, the intriguing of courts, the wreck of great vessels, wars, dramas, earthquakes, national griefs or joys; the strange sequels to divorces, even, and the mysterious suicides of land-agents at Ipswich﻿—in all such phenomena I shall steep my exhaurient mind. *Delicias quoque bibliothecae experiar.* Tragedy, comedy, chivalry, philosophy will be mine. I shall listen to their music perpetually and their colours will dance before my eyes. I shall soar from terraces of stone upon dragons with shining wings and make war upon Olympus. From the peaks of hills I shall swoop into recondite valleys and drive the pygmies, shrieking little curses, to their caverns. It may be my whim to wander through infinite parks where the deer lie under the clustering shadow of their antlers and flee lightly over the grass; to whisper with white prophets under the elms or bind a child with a daisy-chain or, with a lady, thread my way through the acacias. I shall swim down rivers into the sea and outstrip all ships. Unhindered I shall penetrate all sanctuaries and snatch the secrets of every dim confessional.

===Chinese===

如今我既已向那位慈蔼的母亲道过再会，也就无须再等了。我把伦敦四郊打量了一遍，已在「——汉姆」（——ham）赁下一所顶惬意的小别墅，就在这里安家。此地没有车马之喧，没有收成之劳。居民中凡有营生的，每天早晨出门，到别处去营生。此地没有生机之力相汇。此地什么都不发生。日月将在我的身旁流过，带来种种安静事如约的往复。春天，我将凭窗望见小小前园里金链花开放。夏天，杂货铺会给我送来清凉的糖浆。秋天会染红我那株花楸的枝条，再往后，壁炉里的石棉将绽出焰的花朵。布扎德（Buszard）铺子或穆迪（Mudie）书库的马车，隔三差五从我窗前经过，四季皆然。而这还不算全。我会有朋友。隔壁住着一位退职武官，极睦邻风地表示过，愿把他那份《泰晤士报》（*Times*）借给我。我房子另一侧住着一户可爱人家，或许会时或来拜访我。我见过他们在日落时分结伴出门，赶开往戏院的火车；其中走着一位年轻小姐，晚装外罩一件利落的胶布雨衣，也掩不住她身段的动人。或许有一天会……但我抢前了。这些，不过是伴我岁月的暖意点缀。因为我要静观世界。

我将凭窗远眺，金链花与花楸，只作我视野近景里的两抹剪影。我将凭窗远眺，在这般遥遥的隔岸，赏鉴世界远处的盛典。人类将在我的晨报栏里自行列队。生命的任何悸动都逃不过我。政治的纷争、宫廷的阴谋、巨轮的失事、战争、戏剧、地震、国丧或国庆；乃至离婚案的离奇续闻、伊普斯威奇（Ipswich）地产经纪人神秘的自尽——凡此种种现象，我都要以我那如渴的心智浸淫其中。*Delicias quoque bibliothecae experiar.*（图书馆的乐趣，我亦将一一领受。）悲剧、喜剧、骑士风、哲学，都将归我。我将永远听它们的音乐，它们的色彩将在我眼前起舞。我将从石砌的高台上跨上亮翼的巨龙，翱翔而去，向奥林匹斯开战。我将从群山之巅俯冲入幽秘的深谷，把侏儒们尖声咒骂着赶回他们的洞穴。我也许会一时兴起，漫游无穷无尽的苑囿，那里的鹿卧在角影攒簇的荫下，又轻轻掠过草地逃开；在榆树下与白衣的先知们切切私语，或用雏菊链缚住一个孩子，或伴着一位女士在洋槐间穿行。我将顺流游过江河、游入大海，胜过所有的船只。我将畅行无阻地深入一切圣所，攫取每一间幽暗告解室的秘密。


===Original===

Yes! among books that charm, and give wings to the mind, will my days be spent. I shall be ever absorbing the things great men have written; with such experience I will charge my mind to the full. Nor will I try to give anything in return. Once, in the delusion that Art, loving the recluse, would make his life happy, I wrote a little for a yellow quarterly and had that *succès de fiasco* which is always given to a young writer of talent. But the stress of creation soon overwhelmed me. Only Art with a capital *H* gives any consolations to her henchmen. And I, who crave no knighthood, shall write no more. I shall write no more. Already I feel myself to be a trifle outmoded. I belong to the Beardsley period. Younger men, with months of activity before them, with fresher schemes and notions, with newer enthusiasm, have pressed forward since then. *Cedo junioribus.* Indeed, I stand aside with no regret. For to be outmoded is to be a classic, if one has written well. I have acceded to the hierarchy of good scribes and rather like my niche.

Chicago, 1895.

===Chinese===

是啊！我的岁月，将在那些魅人的、给心智插上翅膀的书中间度过。我将不断地吸收伟人们的所写；用这样的阅历，我要把心智充盈到满。我也不想回报什么。从前有一遭，我误信艺术钟爱隐士、会使隐士的生活幸福，为一家黄皮季刊写过一点东西，得了那种*惨败的成功*（*succès de fiasco*）——向来有才气的青年作家都会领到的那一种。但创作的紧张不久便压垮了我。只有大写*H*的「艺术」，才肯给她的仆从们些许安慰。而我，既不巴望爵士之封，便再也不写了。我再也不写了。我已经觉得自己有点过时了。我属于比亚兹莱（Beardsley）时期。比我年轻的人，前面还有大把时日，揣着更新鲜的图谋与念头，带着更新的热忱，自那时起已纷纷抢上前去。*Cedo junioribus.*（我让位于更年轻的人。）说真的，我退在一旁，并无遗憾。因为过时即是经典——只要写得好的话。我已跻身优秀文人的班列，且颇中意我的那一方壁龛。

芝加哥，1895 年。

1. 弗里克夫人（Lady Freake）的宅邸。她是当年著名的女主人，创办过一个光彩煊赫的沙龙，「连王室成员到了那里也必定受到欢迎」。新近一部专著的作者断言：「当今许多女主人实在应当效法弗里克夫人——不但效法她对艺术中之『美』的品味，也效法她对谈话中之『智』的讲求。」 ↩︎

2. 欲知此次盛会详尽的始末，可参看《阿尔伯特音乐厅纪事》（*Annals of the Albert Hall*）pp. 102–124。 ↩︎

3. 「泽西百合」（Jersey Lily）。这是当时人们加在美丽的兰特里太太（Mrs. Langtry）头上的一个别致称号，她原是泽西岛（Jersey Island）人。 ↩︎

===Original===

4. Supposed to have been introduced by Albert Edward, Prince of Wales, who, having heard it in Vienna, was pleased, for a while, by its novelty, but soon reverted to the more sprightly *deux-temps*. ↩︎

5. This passage, which I found in a contemporary chronicle, is so quaint and so instinct with the spirit of its time that I am fain to quote it:
 
 “There were quaint, beautiful, extraordinary costumes walking about﻿—ultra-aesthetics, artistic-aesthetics, aesthetics that made up their minds to be daring, and suddenly gave way in some important point﻿—put a frivolous bonnet on the top of a grave and flowing garment that Albert Durer might have designed for a mantle. There were fashionable costumes that Mrs. Mason or Madame Eliot might have turned out that morning. The motley crowd mingled, forming into groups, sometimes dazzling you by the array of colours that you never thought to see in full daylight.﻿ ﻿… Canary-coloured garments flitted cheerily by garments of the saddest green. A hat in an agony of pushes and angles was seen in company with a bonnet that was a gay garland of flowers. A vast cape that might have enshrouded the form of a Mater Dolorosa hung by the side of a jauntily-striped Langtry-hood.”
 
 
 ↩︎

6. By this title his disciples used to address James Whistler, the author-artist. Without echoing the obloquy that was lavished at first nor the praise that was lavished later upon his pictures, we must admit that he was, as least, a great master of English prose and a controversialist of no mean power. ↩︎

===Chinese===

4. 据推测，此舞系由威尔士亲王阿尔伯特·爱德华（Albert Edward, Prince of Wales）引入——他在维也纳听到过这种舞，一时为其新颖所喜，但不久便回到了较为活泼的 *deux-temps*（两拍舞）上。 ↩︎

5. 这段文字得自一份当时的编年记载，它如此古雅，又如此跃动着那个时代的精神，使我不由得要把它引录于此：

「四下里走动着古雅、美丽、奇异的服装——有极端的唯美派，有艺术家气的唯美派，还有那些打定主意要大胆出格、却在某个要紧关节上突然退让了的唯美派——竟在一件庄重飘垂、丢勒（Albert Durer）尽可设计来作斗篷的长袍顶上，扣一顶轻佻的无檐软帽。也有一身身时新的装束，俨然是梅森太太（Mrs. Mason）或艾略特夫人（Madame Eliot）当天上午赶制出来的。五颜六色的人群混杂一处，结成一簇簇，那满目斑斓的色彩有时叫你眼花缭乱——你绝想不到会在大天白日里见到这般颜色。……金丝雀黄的衣衫，欢快地掠过一身最阴惨的绿色的衣衫。一顶饱受推搡扭折之苦的帽子，与一顶恰似欢乐花环的无檐帽结伴而行。一件大得足以裹住一尊悲恸圣母（Mater Dolorosa）身形的大斗篷，垂挂在一顶条纹明快鲜艳的兰特里兜帽（Langtry-hood）旁边。」

 ↩︎

6. 他的门生惯以这一称号称呼那位身兼作家的艺术家詹姆斯·惠斯勒（James Whistler）。我们尽可不必附和起初向他的画作肆意倾泻的毁谤，也不必附和后来同样肆意倾泻的赞誉，但必须承认：他至少是英文散文的一大宗师，也是一位绝非庸手的论战家。 ↩︎

===Original===

7. One authority derives the title, rather ingeniously, from *Ma Chère*, the mode of address used by the gilded youth to the barmaids of the period﻿—whence the corruption, “Masher.” Another traces it to the chorus of a song, which, at that time, had a great vogue in the music-halls:
 
 
 “I’m the slashing, dashing, mashing Montmorency of the day.”
 
 
 This, in my opinion, is the safer suggestion, and may be adopted. ↩︎

8. I received some great and beautiful light. ↩︎

===Chinese===

7. 有一位权威颇为巧妙地把这个称号的来历归诸 *Ma Chère*（我的宝贝）——那是当年纨绔子弟对酒吧女招待的称呼——由是讹变出「Masher」（猎艳客）一词。另一位则把它追溯到一支歌谣的副歌，那支歌当时在杂耍剧场里风靡一时：

「我便是当今世上那位威风八面、神气活现、猎艳有道的蒙莫朗西（Montmorency）。」

依我之见，后一说更为稳妥，可以采信。 ↩︎

8. 我领受了某种伟大而美妙的光。 ↩︎

===Original===
> Amid all he has here already achieved, full, we may
 

 think, of the quiet assurance of what is to come,
 

 his attitude is still that of the scholar; he
 

 seems still to be saying, before all
 

 things, from first to last, “I
 

 am utterly purposed
 

 that I will not
 

 offend.”

===Chinese===

> 在他于此间已然取得的种种成就之中——我们不妨认为，这满含着一种预示来日的从容笃定——他的态度依然是学者的态度；他似乎仍在说着那句话，将它置于一切之先，从头至尾：「我立意已决，绝不冒犯。」

## King George the Fourth / 乔治四世

===Original===

They say that when King George was dying, a special form of prayer for his recovery, composed by one of the Archbishops, was read aloud to him and that His Majesty, after saying Amen “thrice, with great fervour,” begged that his thanks might be conveyed to its author. To the student of royalty in modern times there is something rather suggestive in this incident. I like to think of the drug-scented room at Windsor and of the King, livid and immobile among his pillows, waiting, in superstitious awe, for the near moment when he must stand, a spirit, in the presence of a perpetual King. I like to think of him following the futile prayer with eyes and lips, and then, custom resurgent in him and a touch of pride that, so long as the blood moved ever so little in his veins, he was still a king, expressing a desire that the dutiful feeling and admirable taste of the Prelate should receive a suitable acknowledgment. It would have been impossible for a real monarch like George, even after the gout had turned his thoughts heavenward, really to abase himself before his Maker. But he could, so to say, treat with Him, as he might have treated with a fellow-sovereign, in a formal way, long after diplomacy was quite useless. How strange it must be to be a king! How delicate and difficult a task it is to judge him! So far as I know, no attempt has been made to judge King George the Fourth fairly. The hundred and one eulogies and lampoons, irresponsibly published during and immediately after his reign, are not worth a wooden hoop in Hades. Mr. Percy Fitzgerald has published a history of George’s reign, in which he has so artistically subordinated his own personality to his subject, that I can scarcely find, from beginning to end of the two bulky volumes, a single opinion expressed, a single idea, a single deduction from the admirably-ordered facts. All that most of us know of George is from Thackeray’s brilliant denunciation. Now, I yield to few in my admiration of Thackeray’s powers. He had a charming style. We never find him searching for the *mot juste* as for a needle in a bottle of hay. Could he have looked through a certain window by the river at Croisset or in the quadrangle at Brasenose, how he would have laughed! He blew on his pipe, and words came tripping round him, like children, like pretty little children who are perfectly drilled for the dance, or came, did he will it, treading in their precedence, like kings, gloomily. And I think it is to the credit of the reading mob that, by reason of his beautiful style, all that he said was taken for the truth, without questioning. But truth after all is eternal, and style transient, and now that Thackeray’s style is becoming, if I may say so, a trifle 1860, it may not be amiss that we should inquire whether his estimate of George is in substance and fact worth anything at all. It seems to me that, as in his novels, so in his history of the four Georges, Thackeray made no attempt at psychology. He dealt simply with types. One George he insisted upon regarding as a buffoon, another as a yokel. The Fourth George he chose to hold up for reprobation as a drunken, vapid cad. Every action, every phase of his life that went to disprove this view, he either suppressed or distorted utterly. “History,” he would seem to have chuckled, “has nothing to do with the First Gentleman. But I will give him a niche in Natural History. He shall be King of the Beasts.” He made no allowance for the extraordinary conditions under which all monarchs live, none for the unfortunate circumstances by which George, especially, was from the first hampered. He judged him as he judged Barnes Newcome and all the scoundrels lie created. Moreover, he judged him by the moral standard of the Victorian Age.
